The death of Mr. Spencer Walpole releages a first- class political pension of the value of £2,000 a year.

It success follows the proceedings which are be- ing instituted to test the legality of the sale of in- toxicating liguors at the unlicensed bars of the House of Commons, it is understood that similar action may be taken by the temperance party against mere than one of the Government depart- ments in Whitehall and Pall Mall. 111ost of these pnblic offices, which are neither licensed as public- houses nor recognised as clubs, have refreshment departments in which exciseable liquors are sold, and it is sated that in one case the contractor is a brewer.

Mr. Lough's letter to the President of the Board of Trade on workmen's trains will scarcely fail to convince Mr. Ritchie that there are some serious gaps in the system, if system it can be called. But what will Mr. Ritchie be able to accomplish with the railway companies? That is the question. The companies vow that they can do nothing more,and that a single additional train would be the last straw on their overburdened backs. Meanwhile the accommodation for workmen falls short of the de- mand to an extent which is incalculable, and the block bids fair to become permanent. One of two things must happen. The companies must find a way of doubling their workmen's trains, or a push must be made to lay down electric trams through the main outlying belts of London. Otherwise, the congestion of London proper, dangerous already, will go on until it becomes insupportable. Which is it to be?

The visitors to the National Portrait Gallery this holiday week had the great satisfaction of looking upon Mr. Watts' fine portrait of Mr. Gladstone, which the artist presented to the nation.and which is on view now for the first time. In this werac the great statesman is represented, rot in those mature years which make him familiar to the younger generation,but in middle life, before age bad begun to write its wrinkles upon his features. It is a very noble production, and is a munificent gift, which the nation will not fail to appreciate. Doubt- less other portraits of him, both earlier and later than this, will find their final resting-place in this historic gallery, which in real value exceeds all the art treasures the nation possesses. To us the like- ness of a contemporary which is to be met with in every print shop and illustrated paper is a light thing ; but as the centuries roll on it becomes a treasure of priceless value.

The dozen days' Whitsuntide recess at the Law Courts closes next Tuesday, and among the actions very interesting to the public will be that in which Mr. Hedmondt is the plaintiff. This is the result of one of Mr. liedmondt's operatic ventures since he left the Carl Rosa Company, in which company his appearance will always be remembered by Liver- pool music-lovers. The famous tenor was one of the principal attractions of the comic opera, " The Scarlet Feather," at the Shaftesbury Theatre last autumn. Between the opening chorus of praise by the Press on the beautifully-mounted productien and its abrupt close there was an illogical incon- sistency, which is not at all an uncommon pheno- menon in the chances of dramatic enterprise. Tim piece was expected to run at least twenty weeks. It only ran four.Mr.lledmondt's action is in the first order of the second week after the recess.

Long before the two new expeditions which are projected with the view of making another attempt to reach the North Pole have got fairly to work, a series of experiments, which one of the American geographical societies is about to inaugurate, may begin to bricg forth some results. This is the set- ting affoat a system of drift casks, with the object of determining the direction of the Polar currents. A hundred casks will be set adrift, in sets of five, at various points, commencing north of Behring's Straits. Many of them,it may be taken for granted, will never be heard of again, at least not during the present generation ; but of the hundred not a few should be recovered in a reasonable time at some points in the Arctic Circle. By this means the existence of any current will be determined. The inlet.and outlet of the passage will become known ; hut the course and direction of it will be a matter for future determination. lam glad to ob- serve that the United States Government are likely to give some assistance, and this is a circumstance of which the British Government should take note.

The satisfaction among the members of the House of Commons who took part in the funeral proceedings at Westminster Abbey with all the arrangements made for the ceremonial appears to be complete. One thing was very noticeable in the exchange of views which took place in the lobby before the procession was formed—the almost un- animeus agreement which appeared to have been come to as to the wisdom shown by the Gladstone family in assenting to the proposal for a state funeral. Many admirers of the deceased statesman thought that a quiet burial at Ilawarden would best have accorded with the essential simplicity of his life. But the spontaneous display of public feel- ing in connection with the funeral, the wonderful order which prevailed, the obvious and genuine feeling of respect on the pert of the vast crowds which thronged Westminster Hall on Thursday and Friday,and the extreme tact and ability shown by the authorities in making the arrangements at once simple and impressive won over those who were in- clined to feel any doubt in th• matter; and on Saturday everybody seemed to be agreed that quite ' the right course had been taken. After the cere- mony, too, those members who lingered in the neighbourhood of Westminster were warm in their praises of the dexterity and good taste which bad characterised the conduct of the proceedings throughout, and in this way had averted even the semblence of a hitch. It te so seldom that a great public ceremony passes off without criticisra and grumbling that this unanimity of appreciation is worth putting on record.

The good old Tory stalwart, " Blackwood's Maga- zine," can no more than the rest of us see wherein lies the justification of the extraordinary triumph claimed as regards China by the Prime Minister and his nephew, " Ebony finds Lord Salisbury's Prim- rose League speech "the most disheartening, indeed the most alarming, of all," and it cannot even con- sole itself by thinking that" the future conduct of the nation's affairs—in the extreme East at all events—will be more satisfactory than the past has been." If this bad appeared in a Radical mcgszine what flat blasphemy of Downing-street it would have been. " Followers of the Government my usefully remind themselves that there are things in heaven and earth more precious than a seat in Par- liament ; that there is a higher allegiance than that 6f party—a paramount duty to the people whoqe welfare is so largely in the keeping of their repre- sentatives." Why this looks like a Christopher North or a Lockhart redivivus, as far as slashing goes, and with somewhat more reason in it.

A useful and in some districts necessary branch of technical instruction has been supplied,by means of travelling vans,for practical teaching in farriery, and a return which has been prepared for the Dur- ham County Education Committee give i some in- teresting particulars on the subject. Only eight counties out of 47 to which inquiries were ad. dressed supplied instructions by means of travelling vans last year, and those were all in the South of England. The original cost of ei van and its equip- ment varies from .£65 in 13erloshire to £l2O in Bent and Surrey, and the salaries of instructors, includ- ing assistants, from £l3O to .£2OO per annum. The duration of each course is invariably ten days, the school being opened from two to four hours each day. As a rule four students attend, the total en- tries for the year varying between 65 and 100. Compared with the cost,the number of pupils taught is small, but in each case the results are held to justify the expenditure. In other counties, such as that of Lancaster, farriery lectures have been ar- ranged, and several are considering the desirability of starting a van.

LADIES' LETTER,.

klir Thus NICLISII,

Canoe-shaped hats with round fronts dented is the centre are exceedingly fashionable, and many are only slightly raised, and the chiffon and flowers piled both on and below the brim, while others are turned up excessively, and curve off to a point, thiq hack folding bows and loops of ribbon or chiffon. The best milliners are favouring very low crowns, and arrange some trimming on the top, rather to the back ; but there are many large crowns shown, and also toreador effects, but these require only a cluster of plumes or a single hand- some feather, held upright by a fancy buckle, and usually supplemented by an osprey.

A very fashionable toque of black straw is of the Tam 0' Shanter style, with a full edging of the fashionable pleated black chiffon. On the left side there are sharp bows of black glac6 ribbon and jetted black quills, held by rosettes of black kilted chiffon.

Very charming toques of quite a small "hat" size are to be seen in fancy straw, and these are lightly draped with sequined tulle, a loop or two of this arranged at front or side, with some fancy wings or an aigrette, and the whole completed by groups of flowers, deftly arranged to show at back and tront. Straws are also sequined, especially the rouleau edge and crown, and then require very little beyond the ehoux and flowers:.

Green straw is certain to be fashionably worn, as it accords so well with muslins, grass lawns, and light textures ,lenerally, and is seen at its best when trimmed with roses. A hat of fancy green satin plait, surrounded by a wreath of pink roses and foliage, with gauze ribbon in tones of green and lilac, and creamy lace printed edge look. well.

A very handsome blouse has cordings running all down the sleeves and across the front and back, with rows of transparent creamy lace in between; this a very effective and becoming model, which can be had ia any colour. A lovely blouse for evening wear can be made entirely of chiffon, over a silk lining, and the transparent material is gathered up into little diagonal puffings, which form points all round the bodice; this model is trimmed with satin ribbons to match, and a jabot of light lace, which goes right across the bodice from the right shoulder to the waist at the

An exquisitely dainty high blouse is made of alternate stripes of accordin-pleated white chiffon and creamy lace insertion, through which a pale green silk lining is seen, and the waistband and collar are of pale green satin ribbon. Cashmere is so exceedingly fashionable that the lighter type of gown, with trimmings of lace appliqué, or sequin passementerie, is delightful for afternoon wear, and -very smart costumes are made of this material. Pervenche, periwinkle, hyacinth, and harebell are all fashionable blues, and less aggressive than violette de Parme, which is really periwinkle with an additional touch of violet, and was quite the rage early in the spring. With these costumes a coat simulating the Norfolk style could be worn.

becoming costume is in rased& cashmere, made with a plain, well-gored skirt, with vandyked applique insertions running down the left side. The bodice is made in pinafore fashion, with yoke of finely-tucked blue glace silk, the high stook arranged in fine folds, and finished with a half. collar of the applique lace, wired at the edge, and then bent outwards in fan fashion. The cashmere over-bodice is plain at the back, and pouched in front, and the neck opening is outlined with the insertion, which runs down the side to meet the lace on the skirt. This bodice should fasten down the side, as the tucked plastron is spoilt if not made entire, but the lining can hook down the centre in the usual manner. The sleeves are irs one piece, with fulness at the top, but fitting closely elsewhere. Short sac coats, with or without yokes, are worn, and many are elaborately braided in brown and gold, or in black, and the sleeves tucked or corded for at least half the length. Short coats are also shown in the Russian blouse style, but with only a slight fulness, the fronts are open, and turn back, with exceedingly smart revers, usually of white silk or satin, with braiding or embroidery. These latter coats are worn with jewelled or fancy belts, and are especially becoming to slender figures. Your, loving JENNIE.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY JUNE 8 1898

WOMEN'S CHAT."

According to present arrangements the Duchess cf axe-Coburg, and Princess Beatrice will speed three or four weeks in London this season, the Grand Duke and the Grand Duchess of Hesse joining them at Clarence House, which is now being prepared for the reception of the Royal visitors. It must strike any ordinary person that either the Royal residences in London are singularly ill-constructed or that a prodigious amount of unnecessary labour and money is expended on them year by year. Marlborough House, for instance, when the Prince of Wales and his family are absent, never seems to be out of the hands of the builder and decorator,while Buckingham Palace, St. James's Palace, and Clarence House,are continually pro- viding work for the men of the brush, to say nothing of their brethren in the plumbing interest. Madame Patti'e appearance— the first since the death of her husband—at the Albert Hall, London, must have been a trying ordeal to the diva, but, it was noticed on all hands, and with considerable pleasure that Madame Patti's voice still retains its old magic, and that her art is unimpaired. Madame PatWe toilettes, of which she possesses an al- most endless array, hail invariably from Paris,wbile her furs—no unimportant detail in the wardrobe of a woman who sometimes journeys to St. Petersburg in mid-winter, or to Canada, and North America, when risks are run of the train being snowed up—are of softest Russian sable, sable,which is worth its weight in gold. Sa far as her jewels are concerned £25.000 is probably a very low estimate of their worth. The diva herself acknowledges a weakness for the pure dense colouring of the turquoise; and without doubt she chooses wisely in naming this as her favourite gem, for the delicate blue is well calcu- -1 lated to enhance her pale fair skin. That the nightingale is occasionally found in the ex- treme North or England is now an assured fact. This year several specimens have again been noted in York- shire. The bird is a trifle longer, and of more elegant form than the robin, with plumage of an olive coleur,on the back and ash grey breast. The eye, so large and bright resembles the robin's. For the first month the bird sings at intervals throughout the day, its song be- ing a sequence of sweet sounds of about 20 seconds duration, and a like interval of silence. In that respect one cannot compare it to the continuous outpouring of the lark, or the broken melodies of the brown thrush. The quality of tone however is exquisite, and one long- drawn swell in it is unapproachable among bird voices. The name of the nightingale is of very ancient origin " Night" being derived from the Anglo Saxon "nacht " and gale from the verb "gallon" to sing. A very fine specimen of the osprey has lately been pre- sented to the Zoological Gardene,which is of exceptional interest, as being probably bred in Scotland, and thus affording practical evidence of the good effected by judi- clone protection in the preservation of one of the finest iiritieh birds: It was thought for some time that per- secutien had effectually driven away this species from our isles but it is now certain that a few pairs still breed in Scotland, though the precise localities are of course kept secret. It is no longer possible for any woman to plead ignor- ance ae to the cruelty involved in wearing birds, and egret plumes for one can hardly take up a paper with- out reading something heartrending on the subject. It 1 is true the women who patronise this particular form of ornamentation (?) are now, happily, in the minority, still one would like to feel that such a barbarous mode had ceased to exist. The Princess of Wales never by any chance permits any form of trimming that has involved cruelty to living creatures, to adorn her headgear, and id this instance, as indeed in many others, we cannot do better than follow closely in her footsteps. The socalled osprey lathe sea-eagle, or fish hawk. The bird whose feathers it would seem are bedispensible to the adornment of some fashionable woman, iretthe egret —a snow-white crane, whose plumage is only in per- fection at its breeding season. From the wholesale slaughter of this elegant creature, and consequeut star- vation of its young, the species ere long will be extinct. And so also will that glorious crow,the bird of Paradise. It is a fortunate circumstance that most of our birds' plumage is of a homely brown or black. It is probably to this we owe the song of the merle and maxis, the lark and linnet. The robin 'mainly on acoouns of that patch of vermillion has for some time figured in hats and bonnets and so has the blue barred wing of the jay. It is fortunate for the farmer that his friends—the rook, dews and starling—are of sable hue, and not white and capable of taking a dye. FOT:SOMP years past we have heard with the utmost regularity, that spots and stripes are to go out, but each eeeson they appear in moee fascinating array than ever. White tulle,lesse, or gauze, with spots of black chenille is the trimming of the moment, for all sorts and con- ditions of headgear, a matter to congratulate ourselves upon. These fabrics drape themselves, and with the addition of a few Illwers, or a little fruit (cherries pre- ferable) the trimming of a hat or toque shculd not be a difficult task to accomplish. The quaint ribbon embroidery on silk or satin, a re- vival of the work done by our great grandmethere. has "taken hold " orce more. It is very pretty, very effec- tive, and not nearly so difficult as it looks. And not only are flowers portrayed by means of the ribbons, but scroll work is done with them, not laid On flat, but ap- parently gasified and firmly secured to the silk or satin by invisible stitches between each little ripple of ribbon. Pete ribbons are gathered and set on with a fine silk cord like miniature frills outlining flowery designs. The needlework of Greece, which has lately found its way to our shores Is unique, and worked mostly on the cotton crepe of the country. It is bright in celocr, but not garish, and very finely wrought, the stitch being cord-like and carried out in silk, or occasionally in coloured cottons. The articles are auitable for:afternoon tea cloths, toilet tables, cushions, antimacassars, table centres or book covers. Some of tbe pieces are merely embroidered with a finely designed border but many of them have conventional leaves all over, and a few are worked in filoselle. _._ . ..

- It is very interesting to note how different nations ex- ' press themselves in their needlework. Close besides the Greek embroideries are those of Roumania, Russia, Den- mark, and Britain The Danish work is vivid, decided and effective, and has the appearance of applique, The Russian fa barbari c. primitive.and reckless,in colour and idea. The Boum Wan approaches more nearly to th.- Greek in its fine restraint, but there is a severity of colour, a classic decorum of design in the Greek work, that we find in no other. As for the British, it lacks the individuality of the other embroideries, but it is abun- dantly varied, if not very original. The thrifty housewife who pays eighteenpenee or two 'killings a pound for her tea, would have to rely upon some other beverage for harmless exhilaration if the prices of last century came into vogue again. At one time sixteen shillings would only secure a Pound of the most ind ifferent tea, while t wenty-four shillings had to be pal d for a pound of the best. And with regard to coffee, this rose at one time to the fabulous price of £lBO a ton ! Why a ton is taken as the standard weight is not obvious, but we read that those who indulged in the luxury, drank as much as three or four dishes at a time, which may explain it. Speaking of tea, that always used by the Queen is known as Earl Grey's mixture." Wherever she goes, a stock of the tea, like her bedstead, accompanies Her Majesty. Daticzows Cares may be made as follows : Take on pound of potatoes, one teacupful of milk, quarter Of a pound of flour, one ounce of butter, half a teaspoonful salt, and a teaspoonful of baking powder. Peel the potatoes, boil them till quite soft. After draining, allow them to steam till they are quite dry, then beat them up till they are quite smooth when add the milk, butter, baking powder, salt, and lastly the flour. Mix all toeetber in the saucepan, and allow it to get hot. Flour the baking board well and roll the mixture out on it, about half an inch thick, cut it ia rounds, and bake them in the oven for about twenty minutes. They may be eaten hot or cold. .

CHILDREN OF CUBA: A TALE -OF LOVE AND WAR

ET GRAHAM REDPATH, Author of "The Baffled Press Gang," "When

Things Went Wry," Icc.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS :—Dr. Jam& Cardonna, who has lost money in speculation, Is leaving Spain with his wife and two daughters, Castilia and Coria, to make his fortune in Cuba. The girls both leave sweethearts behind them— Leon and Castello. Dr. Cardonna makes his fortune in Cuba and thinks of returning home with his family. Meanwhile, during a holiday in England, Leon and Castello have made friends with Vigo, another Spanish visitor.

===== 0002601_18980603_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER V.

A NEW FRISKY).

One evening, whilst they were sitting in the in where they had decided to spend the night, Castello Ektentioned the name of Dr. Jacca Cardonna, with clings of pride, because tender thoughts of his -Ausin swam into his head as he drank his_wine.

'Vigo drew his chair closer to the tire, and fixed Ws eyes on Castello, with an off-hand observation: "Dr. Cardonna—pray may I ask who is he?" Leon observed the anxious listener, and a sudden feeling of disgust seized him. "Certainly," said Castello, carelessly ; "why, he is one of the cleverest men we have in Spain?" "But his ancestry ? " "His ancestry is one of the best that any man can boast. His departure is a loss to Spain. "Oh, he has left Spain! People of pedigree scarcely do that," suggested Vigo with a smile and shrug of his shoulders. As the conversation proceeded it seemed to possess uncommon interest, for the stranger. Lleren was now alert. Leon wondered why. "Well, he did not wish to go, but he had no choice," continued Castello.

, "Business men are moved by many motives; but 'must ' is a strong term." "But true in his ease," said Castello. "Well, some men have a higher sense of duty than others.".

Vigo was clearly encouraging Castello to go on. Leon rose, suggesting, with a yawn, it was time for retiring. Vigo coolly lit.a new cigar by way of reply, and Castello did the same. Leon reluctantly left, fearing the turn the con- versation, which he had endeavoured to check, "11-vightonroowthalky roused. e.lii To His suspicions of Vigo Lleren ere thughim it seemed plain that Vigo was a mere adventurer. When the cigars were lighted, and the glasses refilled with sparkling wine, Vigo easily resumed the lead with : "Is the doctor's a political offence ? " "Oh, n 0,,, replied Castello. "I was just about to give you the reason, as friend to friend, and I know you will keep our secret, when Leon inter- rupted us by hurrying off to bed. The doctor over- speculated, you know, and he has gone abroad to seek his fortune. Just fancy a grey-headed man gone in search of a fortune! And he'll succeed, too." "It is strange and interesting. Then no one knows where he has gone? "Oh, yes, we do; he's in Havana. He's a clever fellow, and will make plenty of money there, I'll warrant. Then when he returns I'm to marry his daughter." "That will be very nice," replied Vigo with a smile.

After a broken sleep, in which Vigo figured pro- minently, with his ugliness exaggerated, Leon rose early. Ile was determined they should be rid of this strange companion at the first opportunity, for he began to vaguely fear trouble of some sort from their association with him.

It was a bright morning, and before taking their seats on the coach Leon contrive I to draw Castello aside to ask him the purport of their conversation on the previous night. Leon could not conceal his apprehensiveness, and said testily that Vigo was not to be trusted, and lie feared that Castello had done wrong in dis closing the destination of the doctor. "Not at all," said Castello, with a laugh. "He thinks a lot of you. Why, Vigo is as honest as the day!" "Well, on that we differ, Castello, and we must get rid of him somehow, once we get to London." "You can do as you choose," said Castello, "and I suppose I may please myself about that." "Alter your resolve, for heaven's sake! We know nothing of this man," urged Leon. "I won't," said the other emphatically. "Yore come to enjoy myself, not to bend to yoikr whims and wishes; and my mind's made up." A shrill blast on the horn announced that the coach was ready to start. As they climbed up Leon whispered :"Have a care, Castello; Vigo is unworthy of your confidence." Arrived in London, Leon once more appealed to Castello to come with him to the old apartments, but unsuccessfully. Castello was in a wilful mood, and went his way with Vigo. Only twice did the tr-o friends meet again in London; once by acci•lent, and once by design. But Castello st,:ck to Vigo closer than a brother. His new friend iiad fascinated him.

CHAPTER VI. TIM HERO OF THE ANTILLES.

Leon two years later was ordered to the Spanish dominion of Hayti, in the West Indies, to quell a rebellion. _

The Convention, which governed the native population, had sent out a proclamation of their favourite doctrine: "All men are free and equal." It was a cry that always did, and always will, prove popular whenever raised in the West Indies. Aspirations for regaining the fatherland cannot for ever sleep in the bpsoms of a conquered nation. it was so with the people of Hayti; but the ill- timed proclamation only stirred them to wild thoughts and reckless acts. Tranquillity Was turned to turbulence, respect for law and regard for the general good were deities dethroned. Amidst the chaos created by the struggle between the whites and the free men of colour, the slaves sought to apply the same principles. They were the most powerful of the three parties numeri- cally, and soon massacred large numbers of the belligerents, ultimately becoming masters of the situation. They held French St. Domingo; but only for a time, for a better Government was established. Military despotism succeeded the gross excesses of the revolution. This could only be for a time, and soon the seal of doom was set on one Government, and preparations made for the birth of another to take its place. The Republic of Hayti was the Dame the people gave it, though it only ruled in the 7rench province. Under Boyer—an ambitious and daring son of Gaul—it was bound to grow. In a little space it sought to usurp the Spanish portion of the island. Proud Spain haughtily sent Leon in command of a strong force to suppress the Boyer rebellion. He made for St. Domingo, the capital of the Spanish possession ; but are he arrived the slender garrison had been compelled to capitulate. Boyer, the people's idol, called upon him to lower his flag and leave the harbour before day- break. That was at twilight. In less than an hour the two other vessels forming part of the expedition had dropped their anchors opposite the forts and were commanding a road leading to the residential portion of the town. Meanwhile a letter had been despatched to the Spanish officers who were to be liberated. Read across it was innocent and satisfactory enough to the rebel chieftain ; but to the garrison, who had been informed of the trick, the first half-sheet con- tained important instructions. The letter read as under:

To the Spanish Officers of St. Domingo Garrison. Get yourself in readiness at once to come on board. Come to south east of town and a boat will meet you. Two sailors are ashore there purchasing fresh stores. And you can assist them to bring them on board. By order, Laos CANTARd, Officer in Command.

The Spanish force was released, and a picket gent to see them off the island. Soon they met the !'atiors with the stores, and they led the way into the trap prepared by the picket. The struggle was short, for the escort, though strong, was soon over, powered. Then the heavy boom of a gun in the harbour was followed by a succession of broadsides from the three vessels. . .

Though taken completely by surprise, the occupants of the fort soon thundered back a reply. The naval fire took the better effect; their aim was steady and concentrated, and the gunners in the fort wasted their strength by spreading out their fire on the extended line. Where the flashes appeared they found difficulty in calculating the range, and on findine it many of their shots took the water in the

The native soldiers rallied; but Leon's force, bril. lantly led, outflanked them. Then they fled lila prairie cattle before smoke and flame. Reoccupation and re-strengthening the garrison took up a good deal of time, and gave Boyer, with his scattered forces, a chance to re-form. Though badly beaten, Boyer was not the man to allow so easy a reconquest. He was galled to think how easily he had been tricked, and he was resolute enough to seek a reckless revenge. Like a tame tiger, he had had one lick of warm blood, and it gave him power beyond what he had believed his strength to be. Leon wasted no time. He wanted the business over, and anxiously looked forward to see Castilia. So he pushed pluckily onward. Fortune favours the brave in war, and thus was Leon favoured, driving back the rebel forces and their desperate leader day by day. In each sortie the rebels were dispossessed of some food or cattle they were taking with them in their flight to sustain them in the barren portion of the island. Thus, when they were hemmed in by the moving belt of steel, before which they had been sullenly and reluctantly forced to fly, the rebels found themselves on a long narrow peninsula—a sandy track which the seas in exceptional seasons must have swept—like the letter I, terminating in Capes Cafron and Samana, with Spanish vessels on either side, and without food or means of escape. Leon and his men had an easy task. Three miles of land, the neck of the peninsula, had to be closely watched, so that none could pass. In daytime the place might have been properly posted by two picket parties, for the conformation of the land exposed the peninsula fully to view. Chafing and fretting like wild animals against iron bars, the enemy at length made a frenzied dash to cut through the lines of their foes. Little they knew of the precautionary measures which had been taken to make their escape impossible. At night, when they made their mad effort, the out- posts were quadrupled, and they recoiled time after time, whichever point they tried, until, worked into a frenzy, which lent them all the advantages of ferocity and indifference to their fate, they com- bined for a final savage rush, which must succeed or give them their quietus, far preferable to their present humiliating and hopeless position. Had they broken through the line they had still two evils between which to choose, either to force the passage between the river and an inlet of the sea, or cross the river, at both of which points the Spanish forces were well posted. While leading a brave charge to break up this strong horde, and so allow it to fall in sections on the resisting line, Leon fell, wounded in the side, half senseless, on the marshy, broken ground, amid rank grasses and weeds. When he revived the din of battle was over. Boyer lay dying beside him, and Leon handed him a drink. Boyer, pointing to his horse, said: "Take care of her," and soon after expired. The mare came to her master, sniffed, neighed, and pawed the ground impatiently, and then scam- pered off, leaping over dead and dying as best she could. Leon shuddered at the sight. The rising moon illumined the scene of recent conflict clearly, and then he saw that it was the face of Boyer who lay near. The two leaders had divined each other's presence in the dark, and this was why the conver- sation had been limited to a request and a reply. Leon felt glad that Boyer's horse had stood by the defeated rebel chieftain till he expired, for it helped solnehow to lessen the need for talk. ........-----------------..,-....,......... There was a murmur of revenge, a hurried ,rush, and a shout of joy succeeded a low groan of pain as the surgeon felt the wound. Another cry of gladness escaped unbidden from the lips of many as the surgeon rose, and Leon's body was borne away towards the Spanish tents. The proud-spirited charger walked behind unled, with slow, deliberate steps. The rebel chieftain's horse returned, looked at the receding group, neighed, and, worn out by the fatigue of the past few days, lay down beside its dead master.

name

A rosy flush of light filled the sky and bathed the earth with a rich glow of warm colour. There were marked signs of activity in the Spanish camp, for scarlet and blue coated soldiery flitted about. But there was no noise, and no confusion. A solitary sentinel stood before the commanding officer's tent, where the Spanish colours floated in the air. At last he raised his right hand in salute, and two officers appeared. It all seemed very mechanical from a distance. The figures were those of the senior officer and the surgeon. They went into the tent, and found that Leon was able to sit up with assistance. "Many wounded ? " he asked. "Sixty," said the surgeon. "And killed? " "A hundred; perhaps more." "Poor fellows. Where is Boyer's horse?* "We returned and brought his body in, after wit had attended to you, and the horse followed. It is tethered in the open." "Stall it next my own. Did the enemy break our line? " he eagerly asked. "No, your charge, Colonel, prevented it being broken," said the Major proudly. "Did it? I'm glad. It's just as well this busi- ness is over. It is a rather poor affair at best against such a tattered host." In a few days Leon was riding up and down the lines again; those who had escaped submitted on finding themselves leaderless, and readily gave up their arms when they learnt that Boyer was dead. They had feared Boyer? Soon the island relapsed into a state of quietude. Then Leon took ship to Havana, where he was heartily welcomed by the Cardonnas, who were on the point of returning to Spain for a while, with a view to arranging for their final return, and occupying their former home and position. Leon, too, was to receive high honours for his bravery and swift success, on his return, at the hands of a grateful country. Indeed both in Cuba and in Spain he was now known as "the hero of the Antilles." Havana is a pleasant place, but Leon's stay was marred somewhat by frequently meeting a man named Vargas, whose face, though not unfamiliar, was one for which he contracted a peculiarly strong dislike. Worst of all, the Cardonnas held Vargas in the highest respect and esteem. "I dislike that man," said Leon one day, as Vargas was departing. "And so do I," added Castilla warmly. "Oh, he's simply charming," exclaimed Coria. "And a thorough gentleman," said the mother. "Besides, he's as honest as the day, Leon ; you will learn to like him," added the doctor.

AS T 3 TEIE urAcE CAP.

_ . . . L is tl little disconcerting to learn tile, there are two h iies of men in whoso lionotir a ill ig as- same-4 the " black cap "—men under centimes at death and the Lord Mayors of London. When the Lord ,Nfayor attends the Koval Courts in Stdo c to b! " sworn in," it is part of the etiquette of tints im- portant occasion that the judges should wear the " black caps.' The " black cap' is invested in the mind of the layman with as much mystery as horror ; and even among lawyers there are few who know its history and uses. Itis in origin part of the equipment of the old order of " Serjeants-at- law," and consists of a piece of ordinary black cloth which was intended to be placed on the wig and thus to conceal the" coif" on solemn occasions. It was a point of pride with the order of serjeant, that the coif was worn in the most august presence— even before Royalty ; but in the presence of Death it was thought proper to veil it in black as a symbol of sorrow. The black cap is always carried by a judge on circuit, when attending church in state, or when engaged in hearing criminal cases. It has, however, lost it old appropriateness, and there is now no judge on the English bench who is entitle! to wear it as a serpent at law.—lrrcra " Cassell' Saturday Journal .

Lord Playfair, Chiof Inspector General of Hos- whWi which regret occurred top rnehnaLopulsa zin asc ept the death Lsdyeeonar;tl better knownwas the son egl,ruggeaPl,laaYnfl'iwr.as born a' Meerut, in the same pofitMalsr.iUn preeidmcy, on the 21st of May, 1819. Ills grandfather, the Rev. James Playfair, D.D , was Principal of the University of St. Andrews. At an early age he was sent to St. Andrews tor his education, but appareatly not to hpayi:fvfaLiro.rd Playfair. the University, since at fifteen he began the study of cheinistry under Professor Thomas Graham at the Ander- sonian College at Glasgow. In 1837 he paid a short visit to India, partly for the benefit cot lois health, and oa his return resumed his studies under Professor Graham. who in the meantime had accepted an appoint- ment in connection with the University of London. In 1838 he went to -iessrsto to attend the lectures of Liebig„ whose scientific fame was then beginning, and on his return he undertook the management of the calico printworks of Messrs. Thompson at CIO heroe. In 1843 he was appointed Professor of Chemistry at the Man- chester Royal Institution, the original acheme of which, not then abandoned, included the systematic delivery of lectures on matters of art and science. In the follow- ing year he was placed on the Commission appointed by the Government of Sir Robert Peel to inquire into the sanitary condition of our large towns, acid when this task was finished lie accepted the post of chemist to the Museum of Practical Geology. The preparations made for the Great Exhibition ef 1851 afforded him further opportunities for the exercise of his administrative abil• , ity. fib was a nt into the manufacturing districts to ' secure the co-operation of manuf aeurers and others; he took a leading part in the classification of products, and . the appointment of juries was left largely in his hands. At the close of the Evhibition he WAS made a Cornnanion of the Bath and received an appointment in the house. bald of the Prince C msort. It seems rather odd that he shotud have accepted an appointment which could be go , deseribed,but the work Was probably in his own line. His previous work on the Sanitary Commission had brought him to the notice of the Prince, who was engaged in a sanitary ccheme of his own. The Prince believed he had devised a practicable plan for the pileifiCatiOn and utilisation of sewage, " which," says Sir Theodore Mar- tin, " he subsequently perfected with the asdstanee of Mr. Lyon `Playfair and applied with 50cce49 on the Osborne estate." Probably this explains the character of the appointment. In connection with the Exhibition of 1882 he was again entrusted with the selection of the jurors, who to the number of six hun lead, comprised men of the highest eminence in every part of Dirope. At the French Exhibition of 1878 the Prince of Wales, as Presid ,nt of the English Commission, appointed him chairman of the Finance Committee, which had charge of the general arrangemsnts. On the Establishment of the /Emsington Department of Science and Art be was nominated-joint secretary with the late Sir Henry Cole. Such a pertnership,where both were alike friger for work and distinction, could hardly be exoctel to last long. After a time Mr. Cole " assumed the secretaryship, and Mr. Playfair became Inspector Gmeral of th Government museums and schools of science. In 1858 he left this position for the Chair of Chemistry in the University of Edinburgh. From this time Dr. Lyon Playfair was almost inces santiv engaged On Departmental intioiries incituted by the Government. The suit ibility of British coal for na- val rl pzriprogsaensi,etahtei,ceatetf the Civil Service were among the plague, the Scottish fiseerips, and subjects to which he had to apply his scientific know- ledge and his administrative experience. He was gra- dually drawing nearer the sphere of politics, though hardly as a politician. Admini,tr Jive work, pr9hiblv in a higher position,was the principal object in view. At the Same Um. ,s 0 far as he was a politician, his convic- tions placed him in the ranks of this Libsral party. A. Phi, general eiee,ror, or 1888 he was returned an mernhat for the Universities of Edinburgh and Aberdeen, hil!. academical connections probably contributing to his suc- ceee. In 1873 he was appointed flatrna.ter General,and he held the office till the fall of Mr. Gladstone adminis- tration. At the general election of 1880 he was again returned by the Universities and became Chairman of Ways and Means and Deputy speaker nf the Hones ef nnmoon, He resigned these offia.ea at the beginning of the session of 1.883. and was made a Knight Commander of the Bath. At the election of 1885, piny feeling malting it impro- bable that he would ho affe tO retain his old seat, he offered himself for South Leerni, and was returned both then,in 1888, and again in 1892. During Mr. Gladenneti short Administration of 1886 he held the pea' of Vice. President of the Council on Education. In 1889 he was nominated by the Prince of Wales as a member of the Council of the Duchy of Cornwall, and in 1892, on the return of the Liberals to office, he was appointed a Lord in-Waiting to the Queen. His career has bean almost unexampled for the number and variety of the offices which he has been called upon to fill. His devotion to one single branch of science served him well as a start- ing point; the Government and the co intry had sud- denly become alive to the importance of sanitary reform and chemistry was found to be an indispensable ally, His practical tact and remarkable adaptability, com- bined with personal qualities which must have made him considerate and Amiable, did the rest. He was at the command of the Government whenever a scientific Idcmonstration would help thorn nut of a diffisulty, and there can be no doubt that from first to last his services have been eminently useful to the nation. The deceaaerl lord was Chairman of this Board of Directors of Parr's Bank, Limited, and when his health Permitted he presided at the half yearly meetings of shareholders, which are held alternately at London and Warrington. THE EXAIVIINER, FRIDAY, JUNE 3, 1898. FUNERA L OF MR. LOCAL REFERENCES. G L NDSTONE.

WYCLIFFE CHURCIL-REFERENCE BY THE REV. JOHN YONGE.

An impressiv memorial service for the Right lion. Williatn Ewart Gladstone was conducted in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening. There was a large congregation. Mr. Gladstone's favourite hymns were sung, and the musical por- tion of the service had also special foatures. Tenny- son's "Crossing the Bar" was sung as a quartette, the vocalists being Mr. and Mrs. R. U. Pearson, Miss F. Booth, and Mr. W. 11. Wallington. A me- morial hymn had also been written specially for the occasion by Mr. Alfred Corney, of Warringron. It consisted of five verses, one of which was as fol ' lows

" O'Word of warfare ended, Dear Word of sweet release Earth's battle-chorus, blended With Heaven's eternal Peace I 0 soldier true and faithful. Look upward from the sod, Thy Captain calls thee to His Halls In the Paradise of God."

Two of the verses were sung as solos by Mrs. Pear- son and Mr. W. H. Wallington. An anthem "Happy and blest are they" was sung by the choir under the conductorship of Mr. George Wallington. Mr. W. 11. Payton presided at the organ with his accus- tomed ability,and at the close ot the service played the Dead March in "Saul," the congregation stand- ing the while. The Rev. Joan Yonne was the preacher, and he chose for his text " Mark the perfect man,and behold the up- right : for the end of that man is peace." Psalat xxxvii. 37 v. Be said without ascribing to the great Christian man whose death " the whole world laments "a peace- tion that he would have been the first to disclaim, he thought that those words combined the essential facts concerning him. They knew what was the general scriptural idea conveyed in the term " perfect " as ap- plied to a man. It was not meant to denote absolute perfection. Rather should it be interpreted in that sense wherein Job was speken of as a " perfect and up- right misname that feareth God and escheweth evil." It ' was the phrase, certainly, by which any man might be described who was honestly and humbly walking in the ways of the Lord,for that was the direct road to perfec- tion. It defined any man who was steadfastly on the side of righteousness ; who was completely loyal to con- science.who sought to live in the realisation of God: a man who bad "the strength of ten because his heart is pure !" That was the perfection contemplated by the text. lii a degree rarely if ever surpassed (at least in a man living in the foremost rank of public life) that perfection had been realised and displayed by Wm.E wart Gladstone. Looking at his life,and looking at his death they might fitly say, "Mark the perfect man and be. hold the upright, for the end of that man is peace." Never before, at any rate in this generation had the death of a man of even great and deserved distinction, created such universal sorrow or hia life and character attracted such unstinted homage as in the case pre- sented that day. They had had before them the unique and impressive fact that not only this entire nation but also the great Anglo-Saxon kindred on all sides of the world, and every other national- ity also had been universally moved by a pathetic solicitude concerning the physical condition of one man, and he not a prince or even a peer, but a man of the people. Men had been called to see "how a Christian can die." As they had watched and waited for and witnessed his death, they had recoiled, and effectively realised, the underlying principle and char- acter of the life, and they had unanimously agreed that alike in life and death, William Ewart Gladstone had been true, pure, noble, heroic and Christian. The whole world was acknowledging that in him it had possessed and in its death had lost its greatest citizen. The scenes at Hawarden—along the whole route to Lendoe, in the metropolis itself, both in Westminster Hall, and the venerable and sacred abbey presented a testimony to real human greatness and worth that history would proudly record, and posterity would study with ceaseless wonder and veneration. They had been looking upon a great, unmitigated and unstinted veneration, homage—. even with some an affectionate esteem—manifested towards Mr. Gladstone even by political opponents ; by men who, in the years of conflict both in Parliament and in the country strove and fought against his political aims with, in cases not a few, both unmeatured speech and unscrupulous opposition. Ho had been charged many times with being unpatriotic, revolutionary, un- trustworthy, insincere,and untruthful. He had been held up both in State and Church, as recreant to every high " principle and unfaithful to every selenin trust. Storms of hostility had raged around him, both out of Parlia- ment and in it, both on platform and in the press, and even the pulpit. But what was it that we heard now from the men who were so hotly arrayed against him then ? Nothing but a unanimous tribute of respect, veneration, affection. The rev. gentleman proceeded to speak of the references made by Lord Salisbury, ear. Balfour, and the " Church Times." Mr. Gtadstone,s unique personality had been supreme in every direction. The versatility of his genius, the vastness of his know- ledge, the breadth of his sympathies, his care for all sorts and conditions of men, his interest in all matters religious, ecclesiastical, theological, social, cemmercial, had caused him to touch the national life at all noints. Men of all creeds and classes had been made sensible of his friendly and intelligent regard. He had won the hearts of men, as well as constrained their intellectual homage. Nor was it only in our kingdom that Mr. Gla- dstone was thee accorded such marvellous supremacy. From nearly every throne,and from every country in the world had come cordial testimonies in his honour. What was the explanation of all this P What was the secret of Mr. Gladstone's world-wide fame, and power, and info- ence,and esteemeHoot had he come to be such a very king of nvn, swaying the intellect, compelling the conscience, capturing the heart? Unquestionably be was possessod of many great natural qualities. His intellectual powers elevated him to conspicuous eminence in scholarship, in literature, in oratory. He had by nature, and increased by faithful use, a marvellous power of concentration, so that he could bend his mind to any pursuit undisturbed by the distractions which were around him, and he could transfer his attention from even exciting pursuits or conflicts to quiet meditation and thought, in a way which, to most, was simply impossible. He never wasted an hour. Be could devote the period of waiting for a division in the House to translating Rock of Ages „ into Latin. He had neither time nor taste for idling under the common and specious pretence of resting. Rest with him meant change of work. And who should say that he was not in the truer path even in that matter? His mind seemed to be a completely I stored reportory of universal isformetion t was ever ready to his hand. He could readily turn his thought to classics, or philosophy ; to mathematics or theology ; to poetry or ecclesiastical lore. He could discuss with politicians or with servants, with financiers or with agri- culturists with tradesmen or with little children, with ; the same readiness of knowledge and depth of interest and sympathy. He was the life of the social circle, the most marvellous and inspiring talker that men ever heard, and yet the humblest and kindest and most sympathetic of men. All this CAMS largely of his na- tural qualities,and of his havin i".en them dun devel- oprnent,training and exercise. BM they did not convey the secret of his unparalielled Plwer.and his marvellous influence upon the nation and the world. He had also many beautiful moral qualities which undoebtedly helped to give him unusual influence with men. among these was his unfailing magnanimity. There was also in him a wonderful patience and humility towards per- sons with whom he came in contact-and whom many a man in his rank would have nistnisee4 with curt- ness and a frown. But the real secret of his power lay infinitely deeper. It was because he ever had from childhood in Liverpool, boyhood at Eton, youth at Oxford,and right onward through all his marvellous career an overmastering sense and realise tion of God—that be was so great,so superlatively good.- The constant cry nowadays was that the strain of toil and stress of care Prevented the cherishing of religious faith. Men of business the preacher said seemed to lose Godly desire when business and wealth inereased. It was so with too many. . Gladstone's religious life in the midst of the most busy devotion to public and political affairs contradicted the idea that religion could not have supreme and esntrolling place in the life of a busy and even a public man. • Mr Gladstone had left the testimony that he found his sabilaths invaluable and never more so than when in high office as minister of State. He guarded the sabbath hours AO far as ever it could be done against the intrusion of affairs. They knew to how diligent a student he was of the Bible ; how he built his faith upon impregnable it,tow he reiled on it as the " rock," and they knew also how firmly and clearly he believed in Jesus Christ as the Incarnate son of the Eternal God. What else than his fervent and profound religion cons' have made Mr. Gladstone the man he was Where else could he have derived that massive moral strengh that fervGur of soul, th;t heroic intrepid courage, that enthu- siasm of humanity, that absolute devotion to truth and ri elite usness, that ardent unselfish patriotism he displayed through the whole of his career ? Giadstone drew hie magnificent strength I will, of soul, of character, from hie contact with Christ. The preacher went on to say that it was Christ who inspired Mr. dladatone with his love of hurnanl•y. The inspiration of his Italian, Bulgarian, and Armenian enthusiasm,and of his schemes for the betterment of hie own countrymen came not from any political Opp ortun- hun,not from any desire to create ferns for himself or even to secure victory for his party, it came from his Lord and Saviour. It wee the strength of a divine faith which created his sympathies. Mr. Gladstone had taught the great lesson to the world that politics were only sound as they were based upon truth, justice, and liberty. Gladstone endeavoured always to reconcile politics with religion. Many times had he 'prevailed

Rut the tributes that had poured in from all naeione tetiCy- inc and homage for him, conclu- sively proved it. Politics, the preacher point' out, to be sound should be based iipen religion. What was wrong for an individual could mover be right for a nation. They would miss po3si- bly the greatest lesson of Glad,tone if they forebore to contemplate his wonderful and beauti- ful death. The preacher drew a picture of Mr. Glad- stone's wedded life which he dEscribed as full of placid home joy with children and grandchildren clustering around him, with occasional visits from the titled and ttio royal. Mr. Gladstane,he said,never lost his place in the natien's thought. The nation held its breath when the first rumour started that the old man was stricken Pith a painful malady which was the beginning of the end. His closing days were charmed and his spirit soothed by favourite hymn and prayer. When they offered him up to him the prayer of comfort and com- mendation he sealed their prayer and sealed his lips and life on earth with a whispered "Amen." On Saturday, the preacher continued, Prince, Peer and people bore him to his tomb in the noblest place of British sepulchre. There his noble countenance and kingly frame were buried out of sight. His influence,his faith,his principles, his example, yet remained, and were quick and power- ful. He joined with the many before him who in this island snry, had declared " the path of duty is the way to glory." But, said the preacher' if possible he spoke with fullest force to the youngernsen,she wing them the glory of true manliness, the nobility of real godliness, bidding them pervade their life, and help to rule !their country, and uplift their state of true religion. He reminded them that he found it possible to begin life in the fear of God ; possible to refrain his youthful feet from the path of sinful pleasure ; possible to maintain religious faith and feeling in an indifferent and sceptical age, and a world hostile to religion ; pcbsible to keep in his latest days a conscience void of offence towards God and towards man ; possible to follow the light and to battle for the right l,aa the end. What was thus possible for him by divine grace, in his exalted station, and amid his peeuliar difficulties, should not be impas- sible for men in the quieter walk of life. The end of that life was pe REFERENCE BY ALDERMAN ROBERTS.

Alderman Roberts presided at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday afternoon. He said he had been asked to say a few words with reference to the great man who had just passed away. It was im- possible for him, owing to the fact that he had only lye or six minutes in which to speak, to do justice to his own thoughts and conceptions of Mr. Gladstone's life and work. And there was another reason as stated by Lord Rosebery, that the death of Mr. Gladstone had been too recent for them to appreciate the enormous space he filled in this country, and they were utterly unable at so recent a date to realise the awful void his death had caused. There was, howev r, one side of his work and character on which he might say a word or two. When they remembered Mr. Gladstone's public life, endowed as he was with physical 'panties of the highest order, and intellectual and mental gifts such as no man of our time had ever been blessed with, one wondered how it was that he came to select the career of a politician as the one in which he could best serve his people and God. There were some people—and some very good people—who thought it was an impossibility for a politician to be a Christian man. Well his (Alder- man Roberts) opinion was that a politician could not be an honest politician unless ho was a religious man. , (Hear, hear). One could elm 'at imagine what a suc- cessful scientist or lawyer Mr Gladstone would have been although he was thankful Mr. Gladstone did not taks up the latter professine. If Mr. Gladstone had become a clergyman he would most probably have been an Arch- bishop of Canterbury long ago. As one who had been associated with politics and political life, he (Alderman Roberts) was glad and proud ot the fact that one of the best men, one of the greatest Christians and the most religious and devout of men was above all an honest (Hear,hear.) He did not say Mr. Gladstone was a party man. There was a wide difference between the two. There were politicians and p but a party man pure and simple was one of:those unfortun- ate individuals who could see nothing but good on one side and bad on another. Mr. Gladstone was not one of those; he was a politician because he believed he could do greater service for the people amongst whom he lived and moved, and could sinearely and honestly worship the God he believed by bringing to bear on the ques- tions of the hour as they affected the life of the masses of the people all the powers be posses3ed,guided bye be- lief in the existence of God and His laws, and trying in every way to bring Christian principles and religious in- fluence to bear upon the politics of this country. What was the result ? In spite of the fact that politics were mixed up with baser passions there was no country so free from influences of that character as our own. The object of his re- marks was to ask all Christian men, all religious men, and good men, to study the life of Gladstone from that particular $ andpoint—remembering that they were placed here for a purpose, not simply to keep themselves inside their own homes and particular home circle—but to remember that they had a duty to perform to others, to their neighbours and tha community at large. Inas- nauch as they had received benefits from the labours of men like Gladstone—a heritage such as no other nation had received before—Se was it all the more their duty that they as citizens, professing to believe in God and His righteousness, should do their duty to make.their civic life and political life pure, to snake it easier to do right than to do wrong, and to make this land a home of freedom, and a home of refuge for the downtrodden. If they only looked at the influ- ence of Mr. Glaistone's life and conduct from this standpoint it would make better citizens of them and in proportion as they became better citizens they would become better Christians. He would like to refer briefly to the kindly expressions by those who had been op- posed to him so strongly. It was a most gratifying thing that Englishmen after all when the hand of death had been laid upon any of them forgot the differences which had existed and remembered only the goodnesses which it was pleasant to dwell upon. One of the most touch- ing tributes that had been paid to the memory of Glad- stone was that paid by Lord Salisbury when he sp-ks of him as a great Christian. N)thing gave him (Alderman Roberts) greater pleasure than to read those words coming from a man like Lord Salis- bury. They all knew the antagonism which had existed between those two men, and he felt hound to say that he believed as thoroughly in the honesty of Lord Salis- bury as a politician as he did of Mr. Gladstone. He be- lieved the motives which prompted Lord Salisbury were quite honest, an i therefore all the more did he respect and admire the testimony which cline from thatsource, and which from Lord Salisbury down to the humblest in the land had been the tribute given of the greatest teacher and politician of this counlry.—ldr. A NATION'S TRIBUTE TO HER GREATEST ,ON.

SOLENIS SCENE Ar WESTMINSTER ABBEY.

The remains of the Right Hon. W. E. Gladstone were interred in Westminster Abbey on Saturday, and received the honours of a state ceremoaial. The City was in mourning—that much was evident after a very cursory survey of the streets. Every building that boasted a staff hoisted a fig at half mast, the whips of cabmen, 'bus drivers, ad the like,sported crape bows,and even the business man, hurrying to his office in the city, added a black tie to his otherwise funereal attire. At eight o'clock all avenues of approach to Parliament-square were closed by strong cordons of police, who diverted vehicle traffic, only allowing carriages bearing members of the two Houses of Parliament and other invited guests to proceed. At the end of Whitehall —Trafalgar-square—the scene at nine o'clock was one of dreary desolation. One is accustomed at this spot to see a crowd of traffic hardly equalled any- where else in London. This morning, however, scarcely a vehicle was to be seen, but instead an ever-increasing crowd surged along the five arteries ' converging on Whitehall, and so on to the route of the procession. In the roadway stood silent con- ' stables keeping a passage free for privileged car- riages, and a long line of hawkers vending all man- ner of memorials. St. Margaret's-street alone was empty; all the rest of the open spacu was requisi- tioned to find standing room.

A VAST CROWD. The roofs, buildings, and balconies of buildings over- looking the square were crowded thick with people, many of them bearing also the inevitable camera and equally inevitable biograph apparatus. For hours some in the crowd had waited, in a few cases watchers standing all the night keeping warm positions of advantage,detertnined at all hazards to pay the last tribute to the deceased statesman. At 10 o'clollir a strong body of Eton College Volunteers,numbering 350 marched up, and were posted along the entrance to the , Abbey as a special guard of honour to one of Eton's greatest sons. Marching four abreaet,flsoked by officers with drawn swords,and wearing a band of crape around the arm of the dark buff uniform, they presented a very smart appearance, and added a touch of colour to the scene. In Westminster Hall itself the weary night watch had been kept up till the small hours of the morning—a vigil disturbed but once, when Miss Helen Gladstone, , Mrs. Harry Drew, and the Rev. Canon Wilberforce entered and knelt for a few minutes at the coffin ot their father and friend. By 930 the barriers had been re- moved, and those destined to take part in the procession had tsegun to arrive. Among the first to enter were the Rev. Stephen Gladstone and the Rev. Harry Drew, Dr. noble, the Bishop of Rochester, Sir Henry Carnpheil- Bannerman, M.P., Lord Edward FitZtTlaUriCe, M.P., Mr. Stuart Worley, M.P ,and Mr. John Burns, M.P. In the Legislative Chamber at the same rime was a full attend- ance of members, including Sir M. White-Ridley, Si' R. Webster. Sir Henry Fowler, Mr T. P. O'Coneer, and Mr. Blake. By ten o'clock Mr. Cnamberlain, Lord George Hamilton, Mr. Ritchie Sir Wm Harcourt, Mr. Morley, and representatives dell sections of the Horse except the Parnellites had arrived, and all wore deep mourning. A few minutes later the Speaker (preceded by the Sergeant-at-Arms, Waring the mace) and the whole body of members

MONODY ON TILE DEATH OF WILLIAM EWART GLADSTONE.

Who would withhold the tribute he deserves? Not honest foeman dare deny his claim To the deep reverence of a nation's grief ! A grief unfeign'd, so solemn, and heartfelt. He strove front-rank'd to aid his country's weal, And gain'd more laurels in the senate strife Than gory warriors gallant men withal, Who planted and fought their battles, and espied To noble titles, palaces, and lands ; He loved his own plain name,—an honoured name,— The name his father gave, with it alone He lived and died, and left it to all time To grace th' historic nage, foremost Among the first, a people's hero ! As orator, scholar. statesman," Grand Old Man," He gains the people's benison, and will Not need the sculptur'd monument, nor To keep his memory fresh in mind, rhe artificial aid of marble group. To give him dignity in loving eyes. To-day's lament will be a growing sense, Of pride of him, throughout an Empire's space, Wherever he is known and priz'd ! Stockton Heath.

J. 31

BALL CLUB, --- ELECTION OF OFFICERS AND COAMITTEE.

There was a fairly-large attendance at the meeting of the members of the Warrington Rugby Football Club held at the Workmen's Club, Market-place, last Wednes- day week. At the outset Mr. Warren prop mad that Mr. George Smith take the chair until the election of president had been proceeded with. Mr. Frank Turner seconded, and it was agreed t He was supported en the platform by Mr. J. E. Warren (hon. secretary), and Mr. Pemberton (hon. treasurer).

===== 0002601_18980603_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUCE TO TRX LATE PRESIDENT.

In taking the chair, the CHAIRMiII said he was sure they all regretted the necessity there was to elect a tem- porary chairman. The loss they had sustained by the death of Mr. Morris cauld not be calculated. He WAS always mcst generous to them, and no one knew better than the Committee and the players what he had done or the cluh --

Mr. WAIMIN said some had thought that this matter should have been dealt with in the report. But that at the best could only be a bald statement, and he thought it would be more sympathetic it the matter were brought before that meeting. It was rather late in the the day to ON a tribute to the memory of Mr. Morris. No one would have been more proud than he was to have been there. and concluded his term of office by takinY the chair and compliment- ing them in one of his well-kaowninimitable speeches on the success of the club. No one bad done more for sport and recreation in Warrington than Mr. Morris. The Gymnasium was a standing monument to his tact,energy and generosity. It seemed to him to be almost base ingratitude that the many who had received bon-fits from him during his lifetime had done so little since his death to keep his memory green. To its credit the club had granted a sum of £2O towards a memorial, which a committee in the town were proposiog te raise. OWint: to the inability of the committee to carry ontthe schomo however, the money was reurned, and it was received with the greatest regret. The club was, however, able to do one little thing which indicated the respect in which they held Mr. Morris, and that was to allow the presidential chair to remain vacant for the remainder of the past soason. (Anolause.) THE REPORTS AND STATEMENT OF ACCOUNTS.

CRITICISM BY MEMBERS.

The Secretary's and Treasurer's reports, which have already appeared in the "Examiner" were taken as read. Mr. WRICIEIT, in moving the adoption of the reports. said they were the best the committee could present, but they were not such as the Warrington Football Club should be satisfied with. His opinion was that the com- mittee had not been as progressive as they might have been. He did not think they ought to be satisfied with the position in the leaeue of sevente or eighth. ZA.t.club like Warrington ought to be more to the front, and the committee should have adopted some means whereby they could have got players whe would have held up the honour of the club. Financielly they had had a very good season, and with the money they had behind them they might have made endeavours to procur better talent. & few years ace they had a 'moon team, but it was thought advisable to drop it the interests of the club. His opinion was then and was now, that they made a great mistake. The did not Herta to have encouraged junior talent i the town as they might have done. Th A.ssociation game was superseding them. Why One of the reasons was that they had robbed the junior clubs of their best players, and they had not recom- pensed them in the way they ought te have done. If professionalism was failed to be the best they should go in for it. There was talent which was latent,and if they had forwarded and encouraged the formation of junior clubs they would have talent by which they emild hold their own against all corners. If they spent LIOCI a year he believed they would he the gainers at the end of the season. If they spent Llet a month on junior clubs they would be more than recompensed by the extra " gate " they would receive week by week. From information he had heard some of the committee had not kept the pro- ceedings of the meetings secret when they should have dene.When a team had been selected and one or two had taken exception to the form of different players he in fee mation was taken outside, and get to the players. It was very unpleaeant for a elm-I:teem; n to be met out- side and asked by a player why he had tried to throw him off the team. Such matters ought to be kept q lite secret. (Hear, hear.) Members of the committee had threatenki to resign for that reason . During the neat season they should get a committee who would keep their own ceunsel and do justice to every player. (Ap- plause.) Mr. W. B/BEY seconded the adoption of the report. Mr. ASIITON said many observations had been male during the pest season regardiug the shorteominge of the committee. As a memb,r of that committee be wished to say that he was not infallible, and he did not think the committee considered themselves to be so. He bad been a member of the club for a great number of years and he felt that he should like to make way for somenee a little younger than himself on the committee. He would not say that they weuld be able to get a more enthusiastic member, but he believed there were some who would oise to come forward. With regard to the action of the committee with reference to junior football clubs, he could say that the committee had assisted them in every possible way. The committee approached the St. Mary's Club, which was the most prominent,and promised to aid them with money by granting them a good match on the Warrington ground, and guarantee- ing 115 in case the weather was unfavourable. The St. Mary's Committee did not look upon the offer with any favour, and seemed to ignore it. The Committee also approached the Latchford Rumors club,and they met them in a very conciliating spirit indeed. He believed they had treated junior foot- ball in the town with every degree of assistance. They must understand that their position in the future would not depend upon junior football. They must en outside for players. (Hear, hear and applause.) They must not be content to occupy a middle position in the Lag e table but make every effort to get to the top. If they introduced outside talent to strengthen the hack division be believed the attendance at the matches could be considerably increased, and they would be able to produce a fir more favourable balance sheet. Last season was a most favourable one for playing matches. Three was not a single Siturday en wnichthe team could not play football. The weather had been excep- tionally good but the succoes of the club had not been i correspondingly great. He hoped f any in em4r had anything to say with reference to the club he would say it now. A great deal bad been said outside the commit- tee, and now that the members lied an opportunity of asking questions be hoped they weuld do so. In con- clusion he referred to the great care taken by the tree- smear in looking after the finances of the club, and especially in chocking the takings at the getes. Mr. GARTLAW? called attention to the fact that the expenses for dinners &c., were £e3 in excess of last year. The Cnuassies Said Mr. Pemberton heti not got his book there.

*

Mr. PEMBERTON said it might be jest the other way next season.

Mr. SMITH maid the players had been more liberally treated with refreshments. There had also been extra meetings at night. If they had one meeting extra each week it made a considerable difference in the amount spent.

I Mr. GARTLAND Said he also noticed that the pay for checkers was in excees of last year. Mr. PEMBERTON said there had been an additional checker at the grand stand.

Mr. GEORQE SMITH (a member) called attention to the secretary's remarks in his report with reference to local talent. He would like to ask whether it was true that deputations had been sent out by the club to report on outside players without result. If they were going to be consistent let them play local men. It seemed t6hina that the club was sitting between two stools when they prated about local men while at the same time deputa- tions were going out for new players. He believed it was trots that deputations had beea sent out, and he thought they should endeavour to be consistent. Ile was of opinion that they ought to do as other Northern Union clubs did,and import good players. o have £3OO in hand and be at the top of the league was far better than having £5OO in hand, and occupying seventh or eighth position. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WARREN said the committee did not send a depu- tation to seek players last season. Be believed that about two years ago deputations were sent out to see 'various players. They spent a considerable amount at money and came back with the tale that the playere 'wanted such an amount of money that the committee laughed at the thing, as they could not afford to pay for 'such players, It was no use fetching inferior men, and good men required such enormous prices that the cons. mittee could not afford to pay them. The reports were then unanimously passed. There were two candidates for the sub-captaincy of the first team,viz, Mr. D. Isherwoodoand Mr. F. Moorer. the former being &Al.:tad. No one had the welfare of the club more at heart and no one was more willing to devote time, trouble, and personal inconvenience to the work than Mr. Evans. He wag sure the committee to be elected that night would givo Mr. Evans the best support they could There would be difficulties to meet during the coming season and be thought they were having an admirable president° cope with them.(Applause).—The resolution wascarrited unanimously.
