JUNE 3rd, 1898

WHITSUNTIDE came too cb. The State in the midst of the natio) Funeral. monrning to be eractly in tt. with the customary festivities of the season. II

is gratifying to the public to have been permitted to read the terms of the touching communication

made by the Queen to Mrs. GLMSSTONE. It was a thorough' y characteristic message, simple sincere, and affectionate, and the nation must this week have honoured afresh the kindly watur, that prompted this expression of sympathy wit the living and respect for the dead. It is not because of her position merely that the English people honour the QuxErr, but because of the tender-heartedness that has made her "weep with those that weep," whether peers or peasant' Death is a ruthless leveller, and, for the

being, Princes of the tilood Royal, peers, polit.. partisans, and peasants on Saturday mornil

stood in and around Westminster Abbey on common footing. In this respect the funersl. Mr. GL&DSTONE Was in perfect consona the wishes of the deceased statesman. death, however, his 'nightly influence

and as the body passed from the precint arena where he had achieved the many of an earnest and purposeful life, to the r Valhalla, within the walls of which ho had a lasting resting place, the deep sense of bi

ment that permeated the multitude of nie was voiced by the passing bell from the to. Westminster Abbey. A remarkable featt..

the extra-mural demonstration was the absoli

stillnags that pervaded the vast throng

mourners, representative of all classes of the col

munity and every part of the Kingdom. Thera

was a painful intensity in the hush which .• upon the people. The problem of unite jag all the salient features of a State funeral with the elements of complete simplicity had been solved with an almeo masterly accuracy. Of straet pageantry there viss none. The few notes of subdued colour_ were fur nished by the robes of the Church dignitaries and the high officials of Parliament. These, however, were the merest pin-points in the vast assemblages gathered together in and out of doors on Satur- day forenoon to pay the last tribute of respect to the illustrious deceased. It would be almost irn- possible to imagine a more striking contrast than that between the elabcrate pomp and circumstance of a naval or military funeral and the sombre and ordered simplicity of Mr. GLADSTONE'S obsequies. To the minds of those who can look below the surface, however, the modulated spectacle could scarcely fail to carry further conviction of the truth of the theory that the national greatoees owes its stability as much to the influence of those who wear the conventional black coat as to those who are enjoined by official usage to perform their onerous duties clad in the gorgeous habiliments of the services, civil, military, and naval. gr- GLAnsroNz's funeral was a striking exemplification of this theory. It was to the most distinguished Commoner of the ago that the final tribute was paid, and with this predominant idea everything was in the most perfect keeping. The funeral it- self was an eminently civil function, but the air. sence of mortuary splendour added to, rather the° detracted from,the solemnity of the occasion. gr• GLADSTONE, incomparably the greatest English statesman of the century, rests where he ought rest, and w'ere the nation demanded that lie should rest, within the precincts of a fane recor nised as the heart and core of national greatness'

Jr is regarded as one of the England, the States and Canada. most gratifying of the many in" dications which have recently been observed of America's wish to stand well with this country that the announcement has just been received from New York of a definite agree' ment for the creation of a Commission to consider all questions of controversy between the Unitea States ami Canada. The agreement is for the pre' sent confined to one point, namely, the formatior of the Commission; the time and place for begitle" ing the work being left for future determination It is the agreement regarding which Sir J-00:14' PAIINCEFOTE, the British Ambassador, and s'e Louis DAVIES, the Canadian Minister of Marin° had for nearly a week been conferring with thet permanent officials a the State .Department Washington. advance in the relations between America and her northern neighbour, and one which a few months since would have been regarded as impossible. That the happy and important achievement is due to the war may be taken for granted ; and that the Canadian-American understanding is one of the first fruits of a revival of the feeling of good fel- lowship between England and America, scarcely admits of doubt. Whether Mr. CueziBEICLAIN'S Birmingham speech in favour of an Anglo- American alliance was uttered with the view to facilitate the negotiations known by the Colonial Secretary to be pending, is, perhaps, not quite so conclusive. No doubt the speech carried with it some weight; and, anyhow, the agreement ties up, as it were, some loose threads that have unpleasantly dangled for years. There have , 'been questions about fisheries protection, alien immigration, boundaries, and alien labour laws. it the diplomatists approached the task which the had before them in the most appropriate aPirit, and with a determination to "disestablish " all sources of possible irritation. Thus it has been found possible to agree to a scheme for the aPpointment of a Joint Commission to which

_ every future dispute may be referred for settle- Ment. Canada and the United States have, in- deed, practically succeeded in thing what England and the Sates had previously tried, and Sailed to do, namely, establish what practically ataounts to a treaty of arbitration. The ultimate ejection by the Senate, of the proposed treaty

Was regarded at the time by many thoughtful men as something approaching to a national disaster; but now the emasculation and final repudiation nf the instrument is not unlikely to prove a bless- ing in disguise. It may be assumed that the creation of a permanent tribunal for the settle- nsent of disputes between England and America Would be the first step towards the formation of that Anglo-Saxon alliance, the idea of which is at Present so enthusiastically cherished beyond the broad Atlantic. That the Canadians would wel- come such an alliance there is very good reason to believe, because though the Dominion remains staunchly royal to the mother country, racial sympathies between the C.3lony and the States have been Would a p developing during recent years. It Pear, indeed, that at least some of the feeling bequeathed by the early settlers in anscla is dying away. Uncertain sentiment is, Perhaps being replaced by practical considera- ei"s• Per there can be no doubt that trade between Canada and the States has within the last few years bean an ever-growing quantity, and it has been computed by a well-informed statisti- cian that one in every six Canadians finds his way to the United States to earn his livelihood. Of ' course, this is a factor in the case which will not be allowed to be overlooked. There is no subject ih dispute between the United States and Canada Which could not be settled by a conference, if both parties thereto are dispo3ed to be reasonable Sod just; and from what is now taking place may grow that solid and enduring friendship, among the English-speaking people of the world which all Britons earnestly desire to see.

A Gladstone Among the many suggested;ways Memorial of establishin2; a memorial to Mr. Gladstone, the proposal n the" Morning Loader," should be lth general approval. We have ro- t lication from the editor of this mr assistance in giving publicity to mutated for 'eracting, by popular itr• sif osenr,t,- • statue to Mr. Gladstone in Par- raent-So Hera, already stand mementoes 4 Gaanl4.: Palnierston, Derby, and Beacons- n—and ,what better figure could to complete the half-dozen than ad Old Man "? None, we aver, ---..arkiaole and widespread demonstration of the past fortnight shows that no man could he Worthy of .higher honour than the illustrious 4tsltesman now at rest within the hallowed walls of Westminster Abbey. It may probably be con- eidered that a statue of this description, alone, Would be inadequate as a national memorial, but that is no reason whatever why the scheme should not be carried out. We think it is almost certain that some plan on a large scale, and which would enable the millions of Gladstone's reverent ad- teirers to participate in bringing to an issue corn- r,lensurate with his greatness, will be adopted ere long. For already b the purpose a large sum has hem,„ been promised, and if a great scheme of Practical utility is put forward there need hardly b,3 any Obtained . limit to the amount to be It is stated that when Parliament re-assembles an influential and representative eenarnittee will be formed far the purpose of taking in hand the memorial scheme, which it is tnooted may be of an educational character. Xevertheless, the proposition to add a statuesque representation of England's greatest Commoner to those now in Parliament-square, is one which ought to be—and we feel confident will be— warmly supported. Fir over half a century °ldansrobrz was one of the moving spirits of the House of Commons, and it seems but meet 4,11 d fitting that his familiar figure should, if only le cold stone, command attention and respect for generations to come in front of the building where- its his greatest triumphs were achieved. To assist this object we shall be pleased to receive and ack- tlewledge subscriptions at our head office, War- tieigton. These will be duly forwarded to the Z'aramittee which has the arrangements in hand. RITNrJORN AND WIDNES.

A JOINER INJURED AT WIDNES. On Saturday, a man named William Griffiths, joiner, was removing his furniture frem the upper portion of his house, preparatory to its conveyance to Liverpool, when he slipped and fell down the stairs. He was attended by Dr. &la ards, who found him suffering from a broken arm; and he was eventually removed to the Accident Hospital. ACCIDENT AT A RUNCORN TANNERY. _ •

A sad acal-ent occurred on Wednesday afternoon by which a young man, named Walker, of Eialton,received severe injuries to his hands, entailing the loss of three of his fingers. It is alleged that the accident occurred by Walker trying to pass 3 pads through the machinery in- stead of two, when his fingers beJame entangled and crushed. Ho was removed to Liverpool Infirmary,where it was found necessary to amputate three of the fingers which were much crushed and mangled. A Wilms MAN COMMITTED TO TEED SESSIONS.

At the Widnes Police Dourt, on Thursday, a young man named Arthur Lloyd, was committed to the Liver- pool County Quarter Sessions charged with stealing the sum of £9 us., the money of his employer, the Rev. Thomas Swift, of Ditton Hall, Ditton. Evidence was given that the prisoner, who has been in Father Swift's employ was discharged on April 13 for drunkenness. After his departure the money was missed frora the cash drawer. He was received in custody at St. Asaph on ' Thursday morning by Police-constable Evans. When charged prisoner replied, " Yes, that's right." Prisoner, who appeared very repentant, said that he took the money in a moment of temptation, and the whole of it would be refunded. A GRUESOME SPECTACLE AT RUNCORN.

On Wednesday afternoon a spectacle of a somewhat gruesome nature was witnessed near the Top Locks, Runcorn. As a boatman was engagad in poling his flat aleng the quay the hook bcame entangled in some heavy object which on being hauled to the surface proved to be the lower portion of a man's leg. The police were communicated with aryl dia,gging operations com- menced at once with a view to recovering the body to which the leg belonged.efter about 2i hours toiling with the grapples the body was successfully landed. It ap- peared to be that of a young man about 25 years of age, and W3S horribly mutilated and in a state of decomposi- tion evidently having lain in the water for some con- siderable time. Upon a collar found at the same time is the mark "Taylor" in marking ink, and it is supposed that someone of that name must have met with an accil int and fallen into the canal some- where in the vicinity ot Manchester,and has been carried down and cut up by the screws of the tugs that ply up- on the canal waters. An inquest will be held in due course, but up to the present the police have no informa- tion as to anyone being ms-g.

SAD DEATH OF A WOMAN.

ALLEGED NEGLECT.

At the Frodsham Police Court on Saturday, Mr. J. C. Bate, coroner. opened an inquest on the body of Harriet Owen, aged 37,wh0 died on the 25th May.—The Coroner said the facts reported to him in connection with this death were as follows: Last Wednesday week deceased was confined, and since then she had no rat attention that a woman in her position ought to have ha l. The doctor (Dr. Burton), in his statement to him.was deity- of opinion that the woman would not have died f she had had proper attention. Under these circum stances he (the coroner) had no option but to adjourn the inq lest, and allow the doctor to make a post-mor- tem examination. Then It would be for the jury to con- sider whether the facts showed sufficient negligence to warrant a verdict of manslaughter. So far as the doctor could say at present, the woman died from exhaustion. That might or might not result from neglect. The woman may have had feed, but whether she could not eat it or could not get it was a question they wonld have to decide upon the evidence laid before them.— Harry Smith, 14 years of age, son of the deceased,eive evidence of identification, His father was a hay-cutter. —The inquest was then adjourned. At the adjourned inquest on Thursday at the Court House, Frodshain, Mr. Bites (coroner), resumed the hearing and the following evideece was adduced : Harry Smith, 14 years of age, said the deceased was his mother and the wife of Samuel Owen, Marsh Green, Frodsharn. He remembered his mother being taken ill on the 17th of May last. There was no doctor present at the confinement, but Mrs. Turner was present. His mother had been ill for a lona time and he used to go and fetch the food into the house. There was bread and butter, and milk and rice, for milk pudding. His father did not fetch any food at all, but brought the money and witnees used to go to the ebop for the things and get them on trust paying for them afterwards. Mts. Ainsworth sent them a pint of milk every day. His mother never used to ask for anything.—By a Juror : Did your mother ever ask for a drop of whiekey P—Wit- ness : Sbe did not like whiskey, sir.—Did not your mother say to your father that it was necessary for her to have a drop of whiskey or she would die ? No, sir.— And did not your father say .6 Go on with your dying then." Witness : No, sir. Mrs Betsy Turner, said she practised as a midwife in Frodsham. s:a visited the deceased in answer to a summons from the little boy, Harry Smith, who said his father had sent for her. It was about eleven o'clock at night and she stayed there until about six o'clock the next morning. Mrs. Owen was confined about 3.30 o'clock. Witness asked then to send for the doctor when she left as she noticed the deceased was very weak. In a conversation with the 'deceased, she was told that for eight weeks she had had no solid food. Mrs. Owen told her the reason was because her mouth and throat were so very bad. She could not swallow anything solid. Witness tried to per- suade her to take a little milk, but it was no use she was such a self-willed woman,—ln answer to the Coroner, witness, said she saw plenty of food in the house of a suitable nature if the deceased would only have taken it. There was bread and butter, milk and brandy and even a little wine one of the neighbours had sent. She never complained about her husband at all. In witness's opinion there was no neglect, but she bad never seen the deceased eat anything. She only took medicine in her presence. Mrs. Mary Ainsworth, wife of Mr. Thomas Ainsworth of Marsh Green, Frodsham, testified to having a conver- sation with the deceased after her confinement. She complained of being unable to eat, and said it was hr inside adding" They cannot make me a new inside." Dr. W. E. Burton, said he was a medical officer and practised at Frodsham. He had been attending Mrs. Harriett Owen from the 10th of April last, for acute bronchitis and general weakness. He called upon her the day of the confinement, but was not present at it. He prescribed several mixtures for her, and ordered a slop diet; but lately he had ordered a little solid food. He could not tell whether his orders were carried out or not; on one occasion he remembered seeing a half eaten pud- ding upon the oven and asking the deceased if she had eaten any of it and he replied that she bad.Ele had also seen some bread and butter about the hens°. It was on account of her mouth being very sore and her gums tender that he prescribed the slop diet, and the change to solid food was recommended because her mouth was much better. Be had never known that she refused any food, and thought that if she did elle should have been forceetto take it. He had made a post martens examine tion of the body with Dr. Selby and as a result he came to the conclusion that the deceased's death was caused by the failure of the heart's action, owing to neglect during the lying-in period. The Coroner : Neglect in what manner Dr. Burton: It is evidently neglect of nursing. I

The Coroner remarked that he considered Dr. Burton had taken the proper course. Whilst the jury were receiving their expenses a bit of a sense occurred. Messrs. W. Youd, G. Rogers and Rotert Rogers refusing to take their fee unless it was for two days. The three jurors protested, and after, being discharged left the court without the oae days' fee, alleffing they were as much inconvenienced as the other jurors.

THE MEDICAL OFFICE WS REPORT..

The Medical officer's monthly report stated the num- ber of deaths registered during the month of May was 22, being a considerable decline compared with the pre- vious month when 32 deaths were recorded. This was an equivalent to a death rate of 15.08 per thousand per annum. The causes of death were from bronchitis three from pneumonia four in two cases fol- lowing influenza; from broncho-pnuemonia two deaths; phthisis and congestion of the lungs one each ; two deaths were due to heart disease, while rheumatic anthrites, :tetamis, gastritis, senile decay, and other diseases each accounted for one death.—The number of births was 38. THE EXAMINER, FRIIIAY, JUNE 3 118.

WHITSUNTIDE FESTIVITIES AT RUNCORN,

Notwithstanding the disquieting aspect of the weather the usual festivities which Runcorn associates with the season of Whitsuatiie were,as far as practicable carried out. These festivities, as is well-known, are ot the na- ture of processions of the several Friendly Societies and Sunday Schools in the town, after which the young people come in for the delights of sumptuous tea-parties followed in turn by games and gambols. The drissl rig rain which fell almost all the day tbrough,withheld many from participating in the annual parades on Monday. Whit-Tuesday, which is a regular tripping day in Run- corn, was kept according to tradition, and large crowds went by rail to Liverpool, Manchester and North Wales, land by sea to New Brighton, Liverpool, and other places I of interest and amusement. " FRIANDLY SOCIETY."

In striking contrast with previous years, only one friendly society paraded namely the " Duke's Club." Assembling at the Big House, Bottom Locks. the mem- bers to the number of about 150, walked through the principal streets,headed by the Runcorn Volunteer Rand, arrayed in full regalia. RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT].

Interest naturally 'centres upon the" great" under- taking which falls upon the hands of the Runcorn Urban District Council, i.e., that of the controling and regulat- ing of Runcorn Heath "The Commons Regulation (Roncora) provisional Order Confirmation Bill " as it is titled by the House of Commons, has been read a first time and is on a fair way to completion ; and when a prominent official of the Board of Agriculture assures us that "every "effort will be made to get the Bill through this Session. It is gratifying indeed,to those concerned. Year in year out, the children of Runcorn, have ever looked forward to their recreation and sport upon the Common ; and naturally one can feel for the youngsters In a season that is a complicated combination of sun. ships showers.

But,Runcorn knows its disappointment at the weather and feels that something ought to be done, and it is gratifying to think that already some good genius of the little ones is suggesting to "go it all over again." The occasion which is mentioned is when the Friendly Societies have their " show"—the anniversary of the Queen's Jubilee—and a more fitting one could scarce be sliwed. Now, you good kind purse-holders try just a little and give the youngsters another chance! Perhaps it will be fine this time, and then they will be satisfied and happy.

The Witsuntide holidays for this year are not yet a thing of the past, though the days for celebrating them are beyond recall. I refer more particularly to the "after effects." Runcorn, for some time past, has had serious trouble with that dread disease pneumonia and not a few of its inhabitants have fallen victims to its ravages; therefore, it behows us to be a little less ven- turesome in respect to the attention, we, as well as our wives and families, pay to that subject we call "dress." Judging from the " summer array ' with which a great many Runcornians badecked themselves during the fes- tiveroccasio ; there may yet be occasion to say they n but " too well." dressed not "wisely "

One cannot help reverting to the dangers of rambling in the vicinity of the canals, for the danger is brought vividly before us by the shocking catastrophe which must have occurred some i•im, s, as exemplified by the discovery of part of a man's body in the Bridgewater Canal on Wednesday evening. It is ell a matter of con- jecture as to how the poor fellow got there, but, at the same time,it is prudent and timely to draw a lesson from the sad occnrrence. Indeed, greater precautions might be taken in the way of going to near, and the canal tow path might be less frequently used by the public as " short cut "to " this Mace or that" with more pro- fit and less risk to life and limb.

WESTON POINT.

THE CASTNER-KELLNEH ALKALI COMLANY. -- The second annual general meeting of the shareholders of the Castner-Kellner Alkali Company was held in London on Friday. Mr- William Mather was in the chair, and in moving the adoption of the report, he said they had made a profit of £9,181, with practically only a quarter of their enterprise at work, namely, an inst4llation of 1,000 horse power. Their earnings were limited to little more than halt a year after they had begun the trade account. The profits were so satisfactory that the di- rectors had gone forward with a good heart in complet- ing the rest of the ep:erprise. The report showed what he might call a remarkable .development of a new pro- nem, never tried before, which had been brought very quickly to a profitable stage. R might remark that they had made very satisfactory arrangements for the disposal of their product,t.le greater part of it being sold before it was made. The . amount of money spent on the works was larger than anticipated, but the works had been erected in such a way as would give them full con- trol over the manufacture for some years to come'. At ma meeting in Oatober he hoped to be able to toll the shareholders that they could pay a dividend at the rate of 8 per cent per annum.—The motion for the adoption of the report was seconded by Sir Henry Roscoe, and carried unanimously.

LOCAL VOLUNTEERS UNDER CAN

UNPLEASANT EXPERIENCES.

Whit-week has now become associated in Volun- teer circles with the annual encampment at some seaside resort. The week's cessation from business pursuits is looked forward tq with pleasure by most Volunteers, but the enjoyment of those under can- vas largely depends upon fine weather. With a bright sun and cloudless sky overhead,and a gentle sea breeze fanning the face, camp life may be said to be idyllic, but when the elements are unfavour- able, and the anticipated fine weather gives place to a boisterous gale accompanied by heavy rain,such as was experienced in the early part of this week, then the Volunteer in camp is to be commisserated. It is questionable whether a single week's encamp- ment has previously been accompanied by such un- propitious weather as has been the case this week. The Warrington and Newton Volunteers (comprising the Is; Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales Volun- teers, South Lancashire Regiment) have this year been encamped near the Foryd at Rhyl. It will be remembered that several weeks have been spent on Conway Marsh in brigade camp, but this year a regimental camp was decided upon, and the same field is being used as on the previous visit to Rhyl. It is most pleasantly situated, and is fairly well-protected by a row of sandbonks. Hitherto the men have left Warrington for the week's encampment on a Saturday, bat this year the London and North-Western Railway Company declined to earry out their usual arrangement owing to the enormous pas- senger traffic expected last Saturday. Other battalions were in the same predicament, and the delay, short as it was, caused some annoyance and disappointment. About ten o'clock on Sunday morning, the battalion paraded in marching order at the Drill Hall. Bsfore starting for tne station the men, who numbered 490, as- sembled on the lawn in front of the Town Hall aCnrYl- 1 • panted by their officers, and before their names were called the band of the regiment played a suitable selec- tion of music, the Newton contingent arriving upon the scene under the direction of Captain Patterson. When all were in readiness the signal for departure was given, and the battalion, which was headed by the regimental and tingle bands, the former under the direction of Mr. Thotnas Armstrong and the latter under that of Bugle- Moj or Heston, marched through the large gates thence along sanky-street and Parker-street to ,lank Quay Sta. ! thn,whr...re a special train Was in waiting.A. large number of me„, waffles and children—relatives and friends of the coros—were on the platform, and when the train stkmed out of the station at 11.19, a slight cheer was raised, which was acknowledged by the waving wf hand- kerchiefs from the carriage windows. Rhyl was reached about one o'clock. The men to the number of 550 presented a very soldierly appearance in their brilli ant scarlet uniforms, and crowds of people lined the streets as the regiment, marched to the camp healed by the band, which p'aved"Ged bless the Prince of Wales" and toe" March of the Men of flarl,cl."The weather was fine on the arrival of the train, hut during the afternoon clouds gathered over the mountain tops, and towards tea time rain cornmencid to fall, and continued du:nrig the whole of the evoning wil-hout cessation The wind also blew in heavy squalls. Tile attendance is one of the largest the regiment has had under canvas The Colonel, adjutantonci officers generally .re doing all they Can to foster an increased interest lathe cause in Warringtsn. Colonel Ridsway and Captain and Adjutant F. M. Rooms, who recently succeeded Captain Silverthoene as adjutant, are particularly deserving of commendation, and it is their wish to see the Volunteers a greater force than at present. On reaching the comp the men consumed a hearty dinner of meat, potatoes, and vegetahlss, after which the chaplain, the Rev. J. J. Langharn, Vicar of Padg,ate, conducted a church parade, and offered an address. As the evoning was wet ties canteens were busy. A good nurrib,.r could not resist the temptation to stroll into town, but the majority found the Hsi le of their tents the most comfortable place of abode. Lights ware out at 10.15 p.m.

Atkinsan. The Ambulance Brigade is under the direction of Captain P atterson. whe reoarts that, the healah of the mea gensrally is go, 1. Tile c vclists are under the control of C whin Appleton, and they are ex- ceedingly useful in many ways. Signalling is else well attended to, the instructor being Colour-Sergeant Chap- man, of the Bth King's Liverpool Regiment. The meals of the men are much the same as usual. Breakfast and tea consist of coffee, bread and butter, corned beef or jam ; while for dinner hot joints and vegetables are provided. Very little outside help was introduced, the catering being done regimentally. imp usual mess tents have been erected by Mr. Cotton, of Warrington. The cooking for the officers is done by Mrs. Booth and her assistants, Mr. Smith acting as head waiter, while for the catering in the sergeants' mess the following are responsible '—The Mess P.iesident, Colour. Sergeant Willcock,with Orderly-room Sergeant Bennion as secretary, QuartermaArr-S ergeant A. F. G. Potter as treasurer,and a committee. Captain and Quartermaster T. Hutchinson, ,V.D.. and Quarter-Master Sergeant A. F. G. Potter are, as usual, in charge of the whole of the arrangements for the provision and distribution of the food. Oa Monday morning at 6.30 a.rn, the men commenced work in earnest, the first duty being the Adjutants parade,steady battalion drill being carried on until eight o'clock, when they were dismissed for breakfast. At half past ten the battalion paraded for some interesting tactical exercises. One portion of the regiment marched out into the country some few miles, and took up a good position defending the river, under the command of Colonel RidewaYi The remainder of the regiment, under the command of Major Widdows, formed into an advanced guard with their main body, and proceeded to search out this position, After coming into contact with the advance force a very spirited attack was made and successfully carried nut, and the battalion having jined furnishing advance and rear guards. It a tractive morning's work. The afternoon mwaasrcahemdoibtacinks, was devoted to battalion drill for half the regiment, and the other half was at company field training. aansistieg of attacking a position by companies, shelter trench exercises being also engaged in. Lectures were given on these movements by the captains of the companies con- cerned to their men,and great interest was tnken in this form of training, which was somewhat of a novelty to the men, and- greatly appreciated. An effort is being made to break fresh ground to some extent, and,in spite of the weather a good day's work was done. Foe cold was greater than on Sunday, and the visitors—and there were many from Warrington—had not a very pleasant experience of life in camp. Rain fell nearly the whole of the day, and the wet long grass rendered walking about a matter of great risk and discomfort. In a neighbour- ing eamp the field was flooded, and the officers had to seek shelter in town during the night. The men finished their duties for the day at 5 o'clock, many of them spending the evening at Rhyl, the entertainment at the pier pavilion attracting a large number of Volunteers and other men under canvas. The weather throughout the night was again of the most boisterous description, and rain fell heavily, the bell tents being saturated. On Tuesday morning rain fell heavier than ever. The result was that outdoor work was rendered impossible. Sergeant-Major Sullivan and Instructors Killen and Barker gave instructions to men in the sergeants' mess tents, on guard and outpost duty, Sal. The band also had a good practice, but under cover. Towards half-past eleven rain ceased to fall, and when the sun, which shone brilliantly, made its appearance, many of the men raised a cheer, and a hope was expressed that it was the harbinger of good weather. Every afternoon there has been company field training under company commanders. Captains of companies have instructed the men in shelter trench drill, digging shelter trenches, and also in attack and defence work by Companies in extended order drill. Instruction has also been given in the work of defending hedges and walls. The weather was so in- clement on Tuesday that nothing much was done except instruction in the tents by sergeants and officers. A lec- ture was delivered by the Adjutant to the officers. The officers inspected the men in the tents, and Conducted instructional work under cover. In the afternoon an

outpost scheme was carried out with much success, despite the very bad weather which prevailed. A smok- ing concert was held in the sergeants mess tent on Tues. day evening,the arrangements being successfully carried out by Quarter-Master Sergeant Potter. The concert Was well attended, and amongst those present were a number of sergeants from neighbouring tents. These

included sergeants from the sth Manchester, 2nd Volun- teer Battalion Rlyal Welsh Fusileers, and 3rd Battalion Royal Welsh Failleer.. Songs were sung by the sergeants of the detachments, and a very pleasant even- ing was spent. •Wednesthy morning again opened unfavourably. Rain fell during Tuesday night and continued with slight in- termission during Wednesday. For a short time in the morning there was a brief parade. In the afternoon there was company field training. AttAA and defence was practised by companies with blank ammunition,one company being pitted against another. The proceedings were more or less interrupted by heavy rain, and the men suffered considerable discomfiture by the continued downpour.

===== 0002601_18980603_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
INSPECTION BY COLONEL SPRITT.

[FROM OUR OWN REPORTER]. Thursday was the most important day of the week for it was on this day that the annual inspec- tion took place. Every member af the battalion was expected to be present, and those who were unable to join the camp earlier in the week travelled on Wedneeday evening. Fortunately the weather took a favourable turn on Thursday morning. Dur- ing the night and early in the morning heavy rain fell, but towards six o'clock a welcome change took place and bright and cheerful weather was experi- enced. This put the men in goad spirits and when they turned out tor the inspection they presented such a smart appearance that it was difficult to imagine that they had nad to contend with the great inconveniences of a wet camp. When our reporter arrived on the scene on Thursday morn- ing, the camp was at its best. The numberless bell- shaped tents covering tee ground looked clean and neat as they glistened in the rays of the morning sun. Although the camp was not so prettily situ- ated as the Conway still there was some picturesque scenery in the vicinity. Looking away to the south and to the east, mountain ranges met the view, and to the west was the roaring sea, bidden from view by a row of sand banks which served as a protection from the wind. To the north of the camp lay Rhyl which was reached by crossing the mouth of the river Clewyd by means of a toll bridge. Taking advantage of the fine weather the men had removed their bed cloth- ing ani mattresses from the bell tents and left them to dry in the sun. The rain, it seems, had found its way inside the tents and as the mattresses used by the men rested on the floor they had become damp. The tents were supplied by the War department, and it is a matter for regret that they failed to withstand the wet and stormy weather. By their appearance it was evident that the tents had done service for a number of years, and justifiable com- plaints were made that the War Department might have supplied stronger canvas. The Warrington and Newton men, however. fared better than some others in the neighbourhood. In other camps some of the tents have been blown away, and about 309 man departed from the Manchester Volunteer camp situated . at the north of Rhyl on Wednesday night as they were not willing to stand the rough weather any longer. At the Conway Brigade camp the weather nas also been very boisterous, and the men suffered in consequence. On Thursday it was reported teat, one of the Volunteers there had died. But despite all discomferts the Warrington men were of a cheerful diepositien, and were hoping for a few fine days before the close of the camp. Dr. Patterson stated.in reply to inquiries,that the health of the men numbering 590, has been ou the whole very good, only one man having been sent home through The Inspector of the battelion this year was Colonel Spratt of the Bth and 40th Regimental , Districts, statiened at Orford Barracks During Thursday morning and afternoon he inspected the battalion. The men formed up in parieito in the morning and were suspected in marching order. Afterwards the regiment marched to Rhuddlan, a distance of three miles where a tactical scheme was carried out. The erenerel idea of the scheme, which had been thought out by the Adjutant (Captain F. M. Boerne) was admirably conceived. The idea was that an invadiug force had been landed at Conway and was marching to seize Flint, and a defending force is cantoned in and about Rhyl. The comman- der of the defending force decides to deny the pas- sage of the river Clewyd(which is unsarpaseable be- low Rhuddlan Bridge) to the invaders and therefore posts an oatp oet line between R Iyl and St. Asaph. The Warrington volienteers were formed into two battalions—the left half earl the right half. True left half of the hatrelion under the command of Major Widdows formed a portion of tee defending force and was ordered to protect the river line with outposts from Rhuddlan village to Plas Newydd and Prevent any attempted passage of the river by a determined re- sistance along the picquet line. The right half of the battalion was under the command of Lieut. Colonel J. C. Ridgway,and it was supposed to form a portion of the invading force, and it was ordered to mike a reconnaisance in force and break through the line of outposts near Rhuddlan and endeavour to penetrate to the main body in rear to ascertain its strength, position, etc. Captain F. M. Rooms was detached as umpire, and the carrying out of the scheme was watched with interest by Colonel Spratt. The attack was carried out with great dash and spirit, but the careful disposition of the outposts prevented the attack from suc- ceeding in getting past the defenders. There was some very pretty village ilehting and also an attack on some picceeets placed in the wood. Tee defending position was too strong to, be taken and the attack tailed. After this the battalion formed up in marching order, and the men corn- meueed he return journey to camp. As the floatation crossed the bridge they presented a pleas- ing spectacle. Notwithstanding the hard 'work which the men had accomplished they did not seem to be fatigued. The camp was reached about two o'clock, and Colonel Spratt afterwards addressed the officers and men. He said he had had very great pleasure in inspecting the battalion that day. On ' the whole the manceuvres had been very satisfac- tory. Taking into consideration the wet weather the men had experienced during the week, the: marching order turn-out was very creditable. Ile did not wish them to go away with the Wert that they were perfect. There were some small points which be thought might be rectifi3d. But on the whole he was very well pleased with the manoeuvres that morning. As regarded the outposts .he could say they were very well placed. The sentries were well under cover and not too much exposed. The only thing was that the small force under Major Wtd- dowe was separated rather too much to the left as the attack could only be made from the bridge. He thought they went with toe great a force on the left. The Colonel then proceeded. to refer in detail to the attack and defence pointing out the weak points. He admired the keenness with which they attacked the position. Before .dismtssipg them. he wished to remind them that in these inspections the old form of marching past and small battalion drill were things of the past. He trusted that every effort would be made to work up the small tactical schemes which were the principal things, and that when they were in camp they would carry them out under service conditions. The men were then dismissed, and heartily partook of the dinner provided. No further parades were taken part in during the .day. Before leaving, Colonel Spratt in- spected the books of the brigade, and expreesed his satisfaction with the way they were kept.

A RUNCORN MAN DIES FROM A FALL DOWNSTAIRS. A joiner named John Harrison, 68, years of age, a native :of Runcorn, died at I the Warrington Workhouse hospital on Wednesday evening. The deceased, who had been employed by Mr. Bostock lodged at No. 13, Cloth Hall-yard, Bank-street, and on the night of the 24th May was trying to get up- stairs when he fell and injured his head and right arm. He was picked up and placed on the sofa,and as he remained in an unconscious condition he was conveyed to the Workhouse,where he was attended by Dr. Braide, the medical officer, but he died on Wednesday morning.—An inquest was held by Mr. Brighouse in the Parade Room at the Police Station on Thursday evening. Mrs. IPreston,lodging-honse keeper, Cloth Hall Yard stated that the deceased lodged at her house, and gave evidence hearing out , the facts stated above.—'llp jury returned a ver- pict of "Accidental Death. BEFORE THE MABISTRA.TES.

A DRUMLIN WIFE.

On Friday at till Police Court, before the Mayor (in the chair), and other magistrates.—George and. Annie Ryder, es, Admiral-street, were summoned by Iffspector McAteer on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.. for neglecting their two children. One was a female child 11 years of age a daughter cf the male defendant by a previous mar- riage. The other was a male child 5 years of age, a daughter of the female defendant by a former husband. —The Inspector said he went in company with P.S. Evans to the house in consequence of a complaint which had been made. When they got inside the house the female defendant was very drunk upstairs' and in a coraer of the bedroom, the father of the female defendant was lying on a few filthy rags. The whole house was in an awful state. Be went again the same night and saw the two children who were in a filthy condition. The total income coming into the house was 375. 61. a week.— P.S. Evans corroborated the first witness,and gave some further details. He said the only covering of one of the beds wb..s the remnants of an old blanket.-4 Mr. Rich- ards said the male defendant turned up his money on the Saturday night, and then he would have to go out and get food. The female defendant was never sober.—The Chief Constable said the woman was known to be a habitual drunkard.—The Bench committal the female defendant to prison for three months with hard labour. The male defendant they let off expressing the hope he Would try and improve the home. ASSAULTING A NEIGHBOUR AT WARRINGTON.

On Friday before the Mayor (in the chair) and other magi.trates at the Warrington Police Court Mary Hew. eon summoned Mary Pollard of Longford-street for assault on the I.Bth May. Mr. Browne appeared for the defendant and Mr. J. Longland for the plaintiff. Mrs. Rawson said on Wednesday night of the day in question, the woman Pollard came at her knocked her on the floor and kicked her when she was down. She had never spoken to the woman since last Christmas. In cross- examination by Mr. Browne sbe said the row began about a clothes line which had been put up on the wall of an adjoining house. Another witness was called but did not see Mrs. Howson struck. Mr. Browne said it was purely a neighbours quarrel. Neighbours always quarrelled about children or clothes line and in this case it was about a clothes line. He called for the defence Mrs. Forster who kept a shop at the corner Longford-street.She said that Mrs. Howson had cut Mrs. Pollard's clothes line. She saw the defendant go up to Mrs. Hewson, and shake her and call her "a bad old faggot." There was no kicking or striking at all. Mrs. Fiddler of Longford-street corroborated this evidence. The bench fined the defendant 5s and costs.

EXCURSIONISTS LT BANK QUAY STLTION.-A TASTI FOR

Tom- Arors.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday morning, before Mr. F. Monks (in tie chair) and Councillor Smethurst, William Hill, 104, Edith-road, Birmingham, and Robert Hardman. 6, Bridge-street, Birmingham, both young man, were charged with stealing from some baskets on tho platform of the London end North-Wes- tern Railway Station at Warrington a quantity of tomatoes, valued at 63., the property of the company,— William H. Shakeshaft, telegraph clerk at Bank Quay Station, said he saw the two defendants standing over the baskets, and he called Inspector Blaydon's attention to thern.—.Tohn Blaydon, traffic inspector, said that fourteen Fsaakets of tomatoes were taken front a London train at 2,35 a.tn.a and placed on the platform. About • 3.12 an excursion train from Birmingham to Blackpool ,topped et the station, while the tickets were collected, minute er two later the telegraph clerk called his attention to the fact that several of the passengers were regaling tomatoes from the baskets, while ethers from the train were encouraging them. Witness watched for a while, and saw the two defendants take tomatoes from one of the baskets. He took them into custody.—John Howello, detective inspector,who conducted the prosecu- tion, said he took the lads to his office, and told them that they would be charged with stealing the tomatoes. Hardman said" I only took four. I bought them for a penny from a boy on the platform." Hill denied that be took any. Hardman, before being brought to the police station, gave a wrong name and address. Oa the baskets being weighed, fifteen pounds of tomatoes were defi- cient. —The Chairman said the defendants had evidently taken the fruit on the impulse of the moment, and as, to a certain extent, their holiday had suffered, thsv would bo fined in the lenient amount of 21. 6d. each.—The fines were paid, and the excursionists afterwards resumed their journey to Blackpool. Blelborne's magnificence he admires, but he has been telling the citizens that the bulk of thern are given over to " icblatry worse than the idolatry of the Athenians, sporting,gambling, lust, crime, chicanery, death, doom, and perdition." An alarming occurrence took place on Tuesday at Cork at a promenade concsrt ix aid of the fund for the relief of dis- tress in the South of Ireland. The concert Was held in a cricket ground illuminated with exyhydrogen lights. One apparatus exploded and shot several feet into the air. The illuminating material, composed of naphtha and flulds,burst, and injured all within reach. In the panic which ensued several young people were trampled upon. A number of persons were treated at the hospital, the most merlons cases being those of Mr Walton. Miss Maybury, two brothers named Hirney and a man named Murphy, all lacerated or scalded.

An accident of a peculiar and costly nature occurred inthe neighbourhood of toe London General Post Office on Monday morning. Just as a four-in-hand laden with holiday-makers turned into Newgate-street from St. Martin's one of the leaders reared and fell, striking in its descent a baker's handcart, which it upset, spilling the contents over the road. At the same moment a party of cyclists appeared upon the scene,and. being unable to pull up in time, the foremost struck one of the rolling loaves, with the result that he was thrown heavily, his comrades, numbering nearly a dozen, falling over him Upon regaining their feet the cyclists ap- peared none the worse for their adventure, although one of the machines was badly broken. On attempting to raise the horse it was found to be dead.

STOCKTON HEATH PARISH COUNCIL.

This Council met in the National Schools, Stockton Heath, on Thursday lest week. Mr. Pears ;n oc- cupied the chair, and there were also present Messes. Davenport, Clarke, Mason, Mclntyre, Hayes, with Mr. Bradshaw (clerk).

===== 0002601_18980603_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE_ ALLEGED ENCROACHMENT AT WALTON_ ROAD.

The Clerk said he had seen Mr. Ellison with reference to this matter who told him that he had not written to the County Council. EH (Mr. Bradshaw) then wrote, and Mr. Reeinald Potts replied stating that he was in receipt of the letter embodying a resolution passed by the St.mkton H.ea'h Parish Council with reference to the enclosure of certain land wltt.n the parish of Stockton Heath and suggesting a give and take line. Would the clerk please let him know whether the alleged encroachment was up m one of the main roads repairable by the County Council, as if so, he should require a plan sheaving the same. if, however, the encroachment and give and take line referred to was upon a dist' let road repairable by a Rural District Coun- cil, the complaint should in the first instance be made to the latter authority. With reference to the latter part of the letter asking for the prumission to place pariah rlstices on the notice board at the corner of Ellesmere - road, the county notice boards were erected solely for the county notices, and he therefore could not consent to the application. Mr. Hayes asked who was to go to the expense of a plan.

The Chairman said the Parish Council would have to bear that expense if they replied to the communication. Mr. Davenport stated that Mr. Linnel, the surveyor, told him that be had laid a plan before a committee of the County Council, and the commi-tee decided not to move in the matter.

Mr. Hayes thought th. Council was wasting valuable time over the matter. Ho moved that the question of the alleged encreachment at Walton-road be dropped. Mr. Davenport seconded, and said he got his informa- tion with respect to the action of the County Council Committee direct from Mr. Linnet.

Mr. Mason : I suppose the plan he saw was supplied by Mr. Hedgcock ? Toe Clerk : I know Mr. Hedgcock has submitted a plan to someone. Mr. Davenport : It was handed to me and laid before a c3mmittee of the Counts, Council. The uommittee decided to allow the matter to lie on the table. _

- --- Mr. Mason thought it would be advisable to get that information direct from the County Council. He would move that the Clerk be instructed to write to the County Cuuncii for the information. Mr. Davenport said he did not mind what action was taken by the Council so long as they did not spend mousy on Mr. Mason thought it was a pity to enter in the matter with such a feeling. The mistake had been made in putting the matter in the hands of the District Coun- cil. They had practically given themselves away by doing so. Mr. Davenport: You see it wasn't a matter for the District Council.

-- The Chairman : The Parieh Council's Act says we must first bring it before the District Council. Mr. Davenport said he would like to know what good the agitation with reference to the London.road en- croachment bed been. It had been a great expense on the township. The Council had had too much to do with the encroachment question. Mr. Mason said there was a clear case with reference to the Walton-road matter. A disposition WM; shown by the Bridgewater trustees to meet the Council and to do somethi• g. But the Council had gone the wrong way about the matter. There was no proposal now for a give and take line. Mr. Davenport said he understood Mr. Hedgcock was willing to agree to a give and take line. Mr. Mason said it was distinctly understood at the time Mr. Hedgcock bought the land that the Council would make a formal claim. On the resolution being put to the meeting four voted in favour of it, and Mr. Mason against. The matter therefore falls to the ground.

===== 0002601_18980603_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
THE .COLLECTION OF THE SPECIAL HATE.

The Council proceeded to consider the question of making a fixed salary for the collection of the special rate.

The Clerk said the sum fixed by the last Council was £l2 103 for collecting each special rate.

Mr. Mason: Tha.E.was for two districts—Appleton and Stockton 13,•ath ? The Clerk : Yes. Mr. Davenport : rho special rate is collectel eaeh half year. • - • •

The Chairman: How much does s penny in the £ bring in? The Clerk A little over gn. - - -

After-a sho.rt consultation in private, it was decided in the proposition of Mr. Clarke, sece,scled by Mr. Davenport that the Clerk be alhwed £8 each half year for the collection of the special rate. OLD LONDON-ROAD IMPROYESIENT. - -

The Chairman said this subject was part of the business discussed at the last meeting when it was de- cided that Mr. Pemberton and himself shouln be deputation to see the owners of property to endeavour to get the land required for making a footpath. They saw Miss Tetlew, and she said the owner ef the houses was a confirmed invalid. He (Mr. Pearson) wrote to Mr. James Warburton to see if he could help them in the matter, and had received a reply in which Mr. War- burton said the matter mentioned had been left entirely In his hands te deal with. Up to the preseat there had been an expense in obtaining the consent of the ground landlord of tl 10s. fki, This sum he thoueht ought to , be repaid by the Parish Council or County Council, and , also as compensation for relinquishing a portion of frontage he wonld ask the Ciericil to pass a plan for bar windows to be placed in place of the present ones. He proposed to give up five feet of frontage to enable the Council to get a footpath censtructed. On receiving a favourable reply he would obtain. the necessary signa- tures of those interested. The Chairman paid the offer was a business-like one, and if accepted it would enabls the County Council to make the footpath the same width all the way down from Hose's house to the swing bridge. Mr. Clarke thought that so far as the Council was Concerned they ought to accept the terms offered. The windows suggested would be an improvement, but it was a matter for the Parochial Committee.

Mr. Davenport said h 3 supposed it WAS understood that the County Council would go to the expense of the footpath. The Chairman said Mr. Linnel had promised to sup- port it if the Council would get the land for the County Council.

Mr. Clarke proposed,and Mr. Hayes seconded a reso- lution that so far as the Parish Council is concerned the offer be accepted ; also that the Clerk write to Mr. Peraberton to confirm in writing his offer of land for a footpath, and that the Clerk afterwards write to the County Council on the matter. A discussion ensued with reference to the advisability of obtaining the consent of the trustees of the late Mr. Pearce to allow the County Council to construct b. foot- path on land in their possession. The Clerk said he understood that Mr. Vere Davies was a trustee.

It was decided that Mr, Clarke and Mr. Mclntyre should be a deputation to wait uron Mr. Vere Davies, and lay the views of the Council before him.

At a later stage of the meeting, the Chairman read a communication from the tenants of shops at the corner of Old London-road calling the attention of the Council to the state of the footpath. It was t 0... narrow, and pedestrians bad to get off the footpath. Part of the footpath opposite the shops ought to be paved or tiled.— It was decided that the Clerk should forward the com- munication along with the others referring to Old Lon don-road to the County Council.

HE-ARRANGEMENT OF THE HEILER SEATS. The Cnairman said there had been a good deal of grumbling with reference to the iron seats which were

fixed near the swing bridge, and attention had also been called to the fact that the seats in the isolated parts of the township were being damaged by children. He. would suggest that the two best seats be brought to the swing bridge to replace the iron seats there. After a short discussion it was decided that the two wooden seats be placed near the swine bridge, and that one iron seat be placed near London Bridge, and another one by Red-lane bridge.—Mr. Clarke suggested that the wooden seats should be firmly secured to the ground.— In answer to questions as to the money required for re- moval and fixing, the Chairman said he was of opinion that the Jubilee committee would have sufficient money tofpay the expense. TEE FIRE HYDRANTS.—A TOM:TRITER BRIGADE WANTED. Mr. Clarke called attention to the hydrants which were being provided for the township, and suggested that somewhere near th. Police Station would be a suit- able place to fix them. It would he a good thing if they could train about half-t-dot.n volunteer villagers in the use of the hydrants in case of emergency. It was no use having hydrants unless there were people about who knew how to use them, The Superintendent of the Wilderspeel Fire Brigade would be pleased to give in. structions to a number of voluateers. Mr. Davenport suggested that a small committee of the Council should be formed to got the names of volua- teere,and attend to the matter. It was decided that Mr. Clarke, Mr. Mason, and Mr. Haves form the committee. This concluded the businees.

When putting on the outer cover after having had the bladder out for repairs, no matter how carefully you tuck in the latter, it is liable to De nipped between the wired edge of the cover and the rim, providing you put on the cover before inflating the tube. When you have got all nicely fixed up and are busy pumping,the nipped portion sometimes makes its appearance as a small blob or miniature balloon, and ualess;you are very spry in letting out the air,there is a slight explosion and a burst tube. To prevent the nipping of the inner tube, it should be slightly inflated before an attempt is made to ret the wired edge of the cover over the rim, and this may say applies equally to tyros of the Clincher pattern, which rely on a beaded edge for their attachment. Hav- ing put a little air in the tube, you will find it fits more easily into its place well under the cover, and provided in the cage of the wired-on tyre you work one part well into the hollow of tly., rim at the bottom you will have So difficulty in slipplug the cover over the edge of the rim at the top.

Tbe great Golden Wheel race was run off at BErlin last Sunday. before 20,000 spectators. Great interest W.as taken in the race,as Adelbert Chase had specially gone over with several of the Palace teams. Chase went off very well a first, and lapped Bouhours at the five kilometres, hut later four of his pacing machines broke down, and Soul:lours got clear ahead. Chase broke all Garman records from 10 to 90 kilometres, and finished only 900 metres b.hind Boiihours, whose time was 20. Bm. 11 4-5 sec. Had not Chase's pacers broken down, the North Roder would have won easily. A 50 kilome- tres match betwes Chase and Bouhours was arranged directly after the match.

A novel form of track it is said is to laid at Ham- burg. It will be a straightaway- one, and is to be on the lines of a awitch-back railway. The inventor claims that the public will take to races run on the track, and has evidently convinced several capitalists who are tak- ing up the iaea. The track should afford a short-distance race and hill-climbing contest all in one.

Jimmy Mitchael st!ll pursues his successful career in America. Lest Saturday in an exhit ition race at New York he cut off 3-sths of a second in the two miles record, doing 3 min 28 2-5 sec.

The Warringson Cycling Club turned out for a run on Saturday, some of the members going to Chester and 801219 to Alderley Edge.lt was decided to go for a run on Tuesday, but the weather conclusively settled any doubt they might have had on the question.

On Monday the first record under the regulations of the National Cyclists' Union (which stipulate that amateurs shall not be paced or receive any other assist- ance in rides which they may take on the road) was established by A. F. Ilsley, of the North Road Club, over a northern course. His performance consisted of riding 187 i miles in twelve hours.

Several bad accidents r+ccurred on MondaY on the Great Nerth Road, near Potter's Bar. A cy3list named -Salrnos, of Finsbury Park, ran into a vebicle and t3us-

. _ tamed Severe injuries : while north of Hitchin another cyclist, named Watson, of Bri ' ghton accompanied by a lady met with a singular mishap,. A. parcel which had been tied to the handle of the lady's machine became detached, and, falling caused the bicycle to overturn. The rider was thrown to the ground and rendered unconscious for some time.

NOTICE Ti) CORRESPONDENTS.

S. DAVlES.—Friday, the lat day of July, has been Bxed upon as the Warrington Walking Day.

THE WARRINGTON BAND OF HOPE UNION. Dear sir,—l see from press reports that there is a ' probability very shortly of there being another Band of Rope Union in Warrineton affiliated with the Lancashire and Cheshire Band et Hope Union which did an amount of good work a few years ago among the various Sunday schools in town. Now I see from a circular before me that the teachers of the Working Men's Mission Sunday School, Bulk Quay, a short time ago issued a note to parents asking if they would ccnsent to their children becoming pledged abstainers from strong drink. Evid- ently the teachers must be very anxious to safeguard if possible these scholars from the terrible evils of intem- perance. They are to be highly commended for their efforts. lam given to understand that there are over 500 scholars on the books of this school with an average afternoon attendance of 398 and out of this number I believe there were 304 scholars who handed in their signatures to the Temperance Pledge Card. Such a good result is wprttiy of imitation and what the teachers of this school have accomplished may act as a stimulus to others to do likewise. Thanking you for space in your paper. I remain yours etc.,

T. ASHLEY.

NOTES ON -(7-i,A.AES AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICICZT.

The Warrinzton team were again badly beaten on Saturday by the Bootle team at Bootle. It is depressing to look forward week alter week to a change in the outcome of the next engagement only to realise that the result is a more pronounced failure than the last. War- rington batted first, and were got out for a total ex- pense of 73. The best scores made were 15, and 13, by Thewlis and Bibby respectively. On the other hand Bootle for six wickets had 207 runs to their credit, in- cluding in contrast to our food team such scores as : Williams (no 4; out) 73; Price 52; acd Jones 28.

The second eleven accomplished a most promising per- formance on Saturday against the Bootle second string. Bootle batted first. Some very creditable scores were made for tha sum total of 168. Ploughman's 43, Bate's 34, and Wilson's 22 were invaluable. Warrington's innings promised well tipue,h uncomplete. Only three wickets fell. Adamson made 49 runs (not out). Cromp- ton and Morgan were got out for 16 and 12, and Pier. point, run out Los 14.

The Cheshire Lines Engineers on their own ground played St. Elphins on Saturday. The Saints were dis- missed tor 71, and the Engineers innings closed for 114 runs for seven wickets of which Brown's 32 and Irvine's 28 were praiseworthy contributions.

The Engineers 2nd team met with defeat at the hands of the workingmen's Mission Ist team on the mission ground, The respective scores were 33 and 41.

St. Pants on Monday recorded a win of 9 runs against Lovely-lane on the ground of the latttr.

The match between Sankey Wire Mills and Padgate on Saturday on the ground of the former was a win for the wire men. EvaNs, Lavell and Llewellyn were the chief scorers. Flobson did his share in the bowling taking six wickets for N runs. 133yd's score of 13 was the best for the visitors. On Monday at Arley Park, Sankey dis- posed of Arley for 9 runs, due to Kenwright and Simp• sons bowling. Sankey scored 28 for 2 wickets.

St. Anne beat Buckley-street Sunday School on Satur- day. Jolley and Knight made 31 and 23 for St. Anna whose total score was 79. Appleton's 18 was the best wore for Buckley-street who totalled altogether 89.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The Warrington Football Club have this week .adver- Used for a team trainer, and such an early step I can only look upon as an indication of the enterprise which the committee mean to show during the coming season. Previously all the training which has been considered necessary for the players has been suprrintended by the Captain or some other local official with only moderate results. If a professional trainer is acquired, it is quits reasonable to suppose that professional players will soon follow. Training itself would never make the present team as competent as is desired. I feel inclined to give the committee credit for their prompt acceptance of the new order of things which will obtain when the profes- sional rules come into operation.

WA.RRINGTON:

WARRINTON INFIRMARY AND DISPENSARY.

The Hon. Treasurer begs to acknowledge with thanks the sum of .t.:50, less duty £5, legacy by the late Mr. Thomas Morrie, per 3ir. C. Jackson Rol mes. ACADEISY-STREEL SPIRITUALIST SEDN'lrti. On Sunday the platform at this hall was occupied by Mr. J. Kay. of Pendleton. In the afternoon his sub- ject was 'Belief, Agnosticism, and :-piritualism ; or belief without evidence, doubt, and certainty, the three conditions ot religious thought in the present day." In the evening the speaker took for his text, "The Rock of ages." The usual Monday evening meeting was not held.

MUSIC IN THR On Sunday afternoon about 2,000 people listened to the following programme of music, which was well rendered by the League of the Cross Band, under the conductorship of Mr, T. Hynes :- Dead March in 6` Saul" (by request) ; march, " Viva Birkenshaw " (Rimmer) ; selection, "El Dorado " (LI. Round) ; selection, "La Favorita (Donizatti); and .6 God save the Queen." DEATH OF A WARRINGTON MAN AT BLACKPOOL.

At five o'clock on Sunday morning a bricklayer, aged about forty y ears, was found dead in the kitchen ot 73,Ribble-road,Revoe,Blackpool.Deceased whose name is Patrick Meakin, came from Warring- ton fifteen weeks ago, and at about six o'clock on Saturday night was brought into the house by an unknown man. He was then in an intoxicated condition, and upon being asked to go to bed by his landlady he refused, sitting himself in a chair near the fire. At 10 30 the other occupants of the house retired to rest, and at about 530 next morn- ing a lodger named John Mclntyre found Meakin lying dead on the floor on his right side. Sergeant Beaumont, who examined the body, found the head in a small pool of blood, and there was on the skull an incised wound about three-quarters of an inch long but so far there is no evidence as to how it came to be there. GOOD TEMPLAnr.

The usual weekly session of the Temperance Mission Lodge ot the 1.0.G.T., was held in the Academy-street Temperance Hall, on Wednesday, Rro. Roberts, 0.T., presided over a good attendance. Two visitors were present from Earlestown. One member was admitted by re-obligation. The roll of officers was called and responded to. Bro. Mor- timore notified a new lodge at Stockton heath. Bro. Roberts reported having visited the District Lodge Session of S. E. Lancashire at Glossop, and also gave a report ot S. W. Lancashire District Lodge at Southport. Bro. Newman gave an earnest address, encouraging the members to still more active work. Bro. Stanley also spoke and sang a humorous song. Bro. Roberts closA the lodge.

OHMY'S CIRCUS. Mr. Ohmy's desire to cater to the wants of the town at his Queen-street circus is now bearing good fruit, as evinced by the audiences there. On Bank Holiday two performauees were given at 2 and 7 o'clock. Large audiences attended at each perform- ance. The programme had been strengthened by some additions and most of the best features have been retained. Miss Allen and Miss Ohmy are as popular as ever. Alpine St. Clair's table act is full of daring. The Zardoes give a splendid acrobatic performance which is duly applauded. Some fine trick riding is done by Miss Louise and Mons. Tomasso. The clown's comic pony "Nancy Lea" ceases a deal of mirth. Claude ()limy in his" Jockey" act is a great favourite. Cruikshanks brings storms of applause in his musical act, and the living pic- tures are a fine feature ofone of the best programmes yet submitted at this place of entertainment.

WARRINGTON SAILING CLUB. I The fourth race of the above club took place on Saturday,in a fine breeze from N N.W., before about 1200 spectators. The starting time was arranged Ifor 300, and promptly the gun was fired, and a ' beautiful start was made. The Saint was first to crass the line,with Imp close up, followed by Peni- tent. All the boats made for the windward shore. The Imp taking the weather berth led the way to Manor Luck, followed by the Penitent, Saint, and Sinner. On rounding the bend the Imp increased her lead,sailing away to windward in splendid style, and rounding the Pertingtou mark some 25 seconds in front of Penitent. The run down the reach made a gap between the boats, but coming on the wind the boats drew closer together, and looked very pretty, the Penitent pulling up a little on the beat. THE EXAMINER. FRIDAY, JUNE 3 198

SUNDAY SCHOOLS PROCESSION AT

EARLESTOWN.

Under very unfavourable circumstances the annual demonstration of Sunday schools was held in Earlestown on Monday. Though fine, the morning opened dull and threatening, but with a strortz north west wind it was , thought an improvement would take place later on. In this, expectancy was doomed to disappointment.as soon after the procession had started on its way rain began to fall and continued to do so throughout tho day. Ten o'clock was the time put down for assembling on the Market ground, and by that hour all the schools had taken up their positions. The sight was a very impres- sive one, as the holiday attire of the children mingled with che binners and bannerettes formed a very pleas- ing en;.emble. Mr, Thoraas Clare officiated as conductor,and mounted on an impromptu platform he wielded the baton what the gathering of children sang the special hymns se- lected for the occasion, the bands accompanying. Four hymns were sung, and at the conclusion the procession filed off the market ground in the following order : Sutton-road Prize baud,Welsh schools; Primitive Metho- dists ; Parr Temperance Prize baud, Earlestown Wes- leyan s ; Salvation Army and Baptists. Tile route bad , to be considerably curtailed in consequence of the rain, which came down in a most disappointing manner, and , on returning to the market ground, the scholars were ' immediately dismissed to their respective schools. The Welsh school had 50 scholars on their books,Mr. ' Owen being superintendent and Mr. Jones, secretary. Primitive Methodists, 300 scholars, Rev. E. R. Davies, minister ' • Messrs. Dickens and Austin.superintendents ; Mr. T. Clarke, treasurer ; Nl* ors. J. H. Woods and J. T. Austin 9 secretaries. Earlastown Weeleyans,46o scholars, Rev. P. R. Passmore, minister ; Messrs. T. Clara, sen., J. H. Profit, W. Clare, G. Clarke,and G. Warburton, su- perintendents; Mr. P. Chandley, treasurer ; and J. R. Barr, secretary. Salvation Army, 90 scholars, Easign Moyle,minist r ; Mr. C. Hazeldiue, superintendent ; Mr. W. Stephens,treasurer. Baptists, 180 sctiolart, Rev. F. E. Miller. minister ; Mr. E. Evett,uperintendeat; Mr.J. Morris, treasurer ; and Mr. W. Litnond, secretary. It was inte ided that all the children should spend the afternoon in various fields, hut the inclemency of th 3 weather caused a forced abandonment of this idea. Cens.'quently as arrangements for tea had been com- pleted, the children assembled at their respective sch )ols and after regaling themselves with the victuals usual at such gatherings, they were allowed to enjoy themselves as best they might for an hour or two, after which they were dismissed to their homes. Naturally the young- sters were very much disappointed, but in some cases they have been appeased by the promise of afield day later on. It is hoped the weather will then be much more favourable.

GYMNASTIC CFIA 111;IONSHIP AT THE WA 2RINGTON GYMNASIUM.

On Saturday evening at the Warrington Gym- , nasium the second annual gymnastic championship meeting took place under the rules of the Gymnas- tic Teacher's Association; There was a fait attend-, ance. Councillor David Tinnion occupied the chair in the absence of the Mayor (Alderman J.E. Wright). The judge was Mr. F. P. North,and the recorder ot the exercises,Mr. %Hash= An exhibition of dumb- bell exercises was given under the direction of Mr. J. Kinsley, by teams from Wycliffe school and Trinity school,first and second prize winners in the boy's inter-school drill competition 1898. The dif- ferent changes in the exercises were gone through in a most precise amd regular manner. An indian club solo was executed by Miss W. Jones, a member of the Warrington Gymnasium with her accustomed grace and skill. A oisplay of bar-bell exercises was also given by teams from St. Alban's school and St. Paul's school,Beath Side,first and second prize win- ners in the girl's inter-school drill competition 1898. These movements were gone through in a uniform manner. showing the thoroughness of the training by Mr. Kinsley, the director of exercises. The rules governing the gymnastic competition were that each competitor should execute a com- bination of ten quick exercises on the horizontal bar, ten exercises on the parallel bars, composed of slow and quick movements, and ten slow exercises on the rings. A maximum of twelve marks were allowed for each combination. One of the items was a high jump which commenced at 4 feet 3 inches. Three attempts were allowed each com- petitor before withdrawal. That the competitors were good all round athletes was evinced by their exhibitions on the different apparatus, which are admirably adapted for developing skill,strength,and agilitygnere were eleven entries for the gymnastic championship,but nine only turned up to compete The following is the list of the competitors and the marks attained on the different apparatus : Bar. With the exception of the Vulcan Foundry, all the works in the neighbourhood were closed for the Whit Monday holiday. The weather on Saturday was very fine and bright and augured well for a pleasant Whitsuntide, and in consequence many people have left the town for various places at which to spend the short time at their disposal. Monday opened fine, but dull and threatening, and shortly after 10 o'clock lain began to fall and con- tinued without intertnission during the whole of the day and well into the night. Outside recreation was entirely out of the question, and as a conse- quence the publicans reaped the benefit. MARRIAGE OF Miss LIZZIE CLARKE. At the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Earlestown on Thursday afternoon, Miss Elizabeth Ann Clarke, youngest daughter of Mr. Thomas Clarke, news- agent, Market-street, Earlestown was united in marriage to Mr. Walter Baines of Preston. The Rev. R. Davies was the officiating minister, and the bride who looked very becoming in her bridal at- tire was Riven away by her fattier. Her bridesmaid was Miss Baines sister of the bridegroom, and the groomsman was Mr. John R. Clarke, brother of the bride. After the ceremony a reunion was held at the Primitive Methodist schoo:room, when about 40 guests were present. The happy couple left dur- ing the afternoon for Llandudno where the honey- moon will be spent. The presents were numerous, and of a choice and useful character.

- • —• A COLLIER'S DELUSIONS. Joseph Ingham, collier, of Burtonwood, wag placed in the dock et St. Helens on Wednesday as a lunatic wan- dering at large. They bad, he said, placed electric batteries to his head, and they were trying to hang him. Dr. Challenor, who had examined the prisoner, certified that he was suffering temporarily from the ddusion that he was under some electric influ- ence.
