AslAuLr ON A WAR L),INGTON BAILIFF AT EARLEST9WN.

DEFENDANTS COMMITTED TO THE QUARTER SESSION'S. On Thursday morning at the Warrington Occa- sional Court, before Councillor John Crosfield, Michael and Margaret Knight were charged with having on the 13th May, at Earlestown, inflicted grievous bodily harm upon Walter Newton, one of the bailiffs of the Warrington County Court.—Mr. Browne appeared to prosecute, and Mr. 11. L. Riley represented the defendants. Mr. Ria-y said that before Mr Browne proceeded to open the case he should like to suggest a matter of pro- cedure. It was quite possible, if his (Mr. Riley's) in- structions were correct, that the highest paint at which the charge could affect the prisoners was that of com- mon assault. If this were a petty sessioos, and not an occasional court, the offence could he dealt with sum- marily. What was passing through his mind was that the charge might be reduced. Mr. Browne replied that if the Bench thought that oaly a caminaa assault had been committed, and that there was not sufficient evidence to sustain the charge of inflicting grievous bodily harm, the Court could remand to the next Petty Sessions. Mr. Riley said that would suit him. Councillor Crosfield : I cannot judge until I have hearl some evidence. Mr. Browne then detailed the facts of the case, re- marking that Earlestown. was in the Warrington County Court district, and it was part of the bailiffs"' duty to execute commitmeats that bad been granted by the jutge of the court. In the present case a commitment was granted on the 9th December, 1897, against Michael Knight, to commit to prison for 36 days in default of the payment of £l. 91 91. The bailiffs had on a number of occasions tried to arrest him, but could not find nim, and on the 6th May,Newton, accompanied by a police officer, went to the defendants' house. lie saw the female defendant and asked Iser where her husband was, when she replied "You are too late, you will not get him. I will send him away to Scotland to-morrow morning. Why don't you work for a living, like other folks?'' these remarks being punctuated with oaths. She also made use of threats. They did not find the man, and the constable advised her to be quiet, Later on the compninant was in Leghastreet, Earles- town, when he met the male defend ant, and told him that he hod a warrant for his apprehension, an that he was a bailiff of the Warrington County Court. Whilst the officer was getting hold of him the female defendant Mlle up in a fearfal rage and struck the bailiff with her fist and followed the assault up with other blows. The son came upon the sceue,caught hold of the complainant and dragged him down, and he had to release his hold of the male defendant, who, beiog at liberty, to :k a running kick at Newton, breaking a rib. Efe (Mr. Browne)did not know what the defence was going to be, but he had respectable witnesses to prove the assault. Leah-street. Earlestown, was a very notorious street, so much so that the bailiffs were afraid to ge down it, and the effect this prosecution must have upsn the street would be of uotold worth, because the officers of the court had a nasty duty to perform,aad thevn3 orformedit. When Newton came to himself he found himself reared llu against a shop wall. He was in a very we alt condition A lad brooch: him his cap, and he was subssquently EONI by the police. When he returned home Nwr ton had to consult a doctor, who ordered him to go to bed. Pr. Bowden examined him, and found that a rib had been broken. possibly a second one, but the doctor could not say definitely at that time. For a fortnight the corn• Maioant had been spitting blood. The assault, there- fore, could not be a small one, it was one of inflicting grevious bodily harm, Walter Newton, 24, Ashtonaltreet, Warrington, said he had been a bailiff of the Warrington County Court for fourteen months. On the 6th May he went to de- fendant's house and saw the female defendant,who made certain statements in the presence of P.C. Brindle. On the 13th May he was again in Earlestown going down Legh-street with the warrant in his possession. He met Mich sei Knight. and told him he was an officer of the Warrington County Court, and the warrant was for his apprehension for the payment of Ll. 9i, 91. The defendant then asked him, "Could he go home ?" but he said he must go with him to the Police Station. Defendant then attempted to get away, and he caue,ht hold of him by the collar and advised him to go quietly. S me people then got round witness, and someone asked who witness was,and the reply was " that he was a bailiff." At that point the female defendant rushed up in a very excited state, and dealt him, with her right clenched fist, a terrific blow in the left eye, followed be two others,in rapid succession, exclaimina "There, you pig, I told you I would do it for you." Up to that time the male defendant had done nothing but tried to get away from him. He then felt someone place one of his arms round his neck from behind, as he had hold of the male defendant, and be attempted to kick him, when someone else gave him a kick behind the knees which caused him to fall to the ground. He brought the male defendant also to the ground with him, when some man seizsd witness's right arm, and gave it a smart jerk behind him, saying "You won't use that arm again for a bit." In consequence he had to liberate the male defendant, who got up and gave him severe kick in the ribs on time right side, as he lay on the ground. He did not remember anything further until he found himself leaning against a wall with a crowd around him. A boy brought Itis;cap to him, and the defendants had both gone. He than saw two police- ' men, and subesquently came back to Warrington. He was very ill during the night, from pains in the head and spitting blood. The following morning he applie I for a warrant, which was granted. The 93RIP day be Was visited by Dr. Bowden, who had attended to him since. He was confined to his bed a fortnight, and he had been spitting blood during that time, and had suffered a great deal from a bad cough. In cross-examination witness said he had never re- ceived a glass of whiskey from the female defendant for his cough. The day of the occurrence was on a Newton race day, and the streets were crowded when he saw the male defendant. Before arresting defendant he had a glass of Stout and a meat pie in the Glans Barrel public- house, for his lunch, arid was not under the influence of drink when he arrested him. Defendant asked him to go home with him, but witness declined, as it was not their practice to do so. A postman refused to give him ; assistance in conveying defendant to the Police Station. : Defendant's son, when he came to the crowd only said I "Let him go," meaning his father. The crowd did not cry "shame" on him. He did not strike Mrs. Knight at 1, all. Before becoming a bailiff witness was a constable ' , in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee, and the , manager had given him the highest testimonial. fer.Bowden.Bold-street,Warrinaton,said he was called in to camplainant,ann found his left eye blackened. He , complained of a tenderness in his right shoulder and the right side of has chest. Be had a cough, and said that he was spitting blood. Witness examined his side, and came to the conclusion that he had a fractured rib. He then ordered him to bed. The next morning he ex- amined hint again, and found in addition to the in- juries he had discovered the previous day a bruise over the right hip and another on the back. The whole time he coughed incessantly, and that made it diffitult to examine him. On the 16th and 17th May he saw him spit blood on both occasions. That pointed to an in-. jury to the lung from the fractured rib penetrating it tie was in very great pain. For the first few days his life was in danger. He was attending him now, and he was recovering, For twelve or fourteen days he was confined to his bed, and for at least a fortnight he would not be fit to resume his occupation. The in- juries he had described were quiteconsistent with prose- cutor'e statement. Cross-examined by Mr. Riley : The disposition of the marks pointed to him having teen assaulted by someone more than once. As to the injuries on his side, sup- posed to have been sustained whilst !pine an his left side, he thought it probable that the violence had been inflicted on this front of him rather than from behind. The tenderness of the shoulder indicated a sprain. James Thomas, postrnan,ls. Grant-street, Earlestown, said he was delivering his letters at 4.50 p.m., on the 12th May in Legh-street. He saw Newton had hold of Knight by the arm. There was no crowd round them. Newton said to him " I want you to assist me to take this man." Witness replied "lam on duty." When he got to the corner of Sharpe's shop ho saw the female de- fendant come up and strike complainant in the face., A crowd gathered round, and he could not see what hap- peued. As the crowd closed in ha saw the male defend- ant run away. When he passed him he 'said, " Toat's all I wanted, she'll soon settle it for me." He saw Naw- ton walk away holding hia hands to his face. The disturbance only lasted about four minutes. Christina Jones, of 27, Houghton-street, Earlestown, said she saw Newton hold of Knight's coat, heard him ask the postman `Ftiomas to come and assist him. A crowd then assembled, and Mrs. Knight came up and struck the bailiff twice. Police-constable Brindle said he accompanied the bailiff Newton to the aefendant's house. The male de- , fondant was out at the time. He heard the female de- fendant use certain statements against Newton. John Johnson, collier, residing at Legiastreet, Earles- town, called for the defence, said he saw Newton get hold of Knight by the coat collar, bet did not see Mrs. Knight strike the bailiff. Elizabeth , Flemino of Earlestown, another witness car- e roborated all the previous witness said. Joseph Phillips, fish salesman, Tamworth-street, Earlestawn. said he was Corning from the races on 13th May. Newton had Knight by the coat with the tso hands, Be also saw Mrs. Knight come upon the scene and make a " crack " at the bailiff, with the right ha nil. ' Michael Knight,on being sworn. said " all I have to say was what I told the police sergeant, that I never ' either struck or kicked the bailiff." Mary Knight said, " all I have to say is that I went to rescue my husband from being pulled about the roads. My husband neither struck nor kicked him." Mr. Riley said he saw nothing to make it a ease of ' "inflicting grevious bodily harm." It was merely one of common assault. The prisoners were committed to the Lverpool Quar- ter Sessions, and were released on bail.

THE CHURCH DIFFERENCES AT

RAYDOCK.

THE PARISHIONER'S WARDEN.

_ In consequence of the f act le it the election of Mr. John Robinson as Parishioner'a Warden for the parish of St. James', Haydock, was not legally condacted, a meet- ing for the purpose of making a legal selection was called by the Vicar (the Rev. F. J. Ireland) on Thursday even- ing. The meeting was held in the National Sehoolrsom, and was presided over by the Ray. F. J. Ireland. There was a very largo attendance of parishioners. The Vicar stated that this was a resumption of the vestry meeting which was abruptly terminated at Easter, , and they could now proceed with the election of a parishioner's warden. Mr. Hilton moved the appointment of Mr. J. Robinson as people's warden. He proceeded to refer to acts in the Church to which objection had been taken, and said the Archdeacon had told the Vicar he was breaking the law in wearing certain vestments and using certain cere- monials.

The Vicar said he did not. They had all this up be- fore at previous meetings. What wa i the use of wasting time? Was there any good in bringing thorn up to-night? Mr. Hilton contended there was good in bringing these things to light. Mr. Robinson said he would have to refer to them if they were going to pursuade him to b 3 people's warden.

The Vicar : All right go on with the argument. Mr. Hilton said he had to ask Mr. Robinson it he vrai prepared t continue the work which he had begun pro- perly, and was he prepared not to compromise them without giving them some intimation on the p Ant. They were not going to b 3 compromised on any point ; it was not to be peace at any prioe ; they were going to accept nothing less than sixteen ounces to the Pound from the pulpit, and nothing but Protestantism in the Church.

Mr. R. Gregory seconded. Mr. Robinson after a lengthy statement bearing chiefly upon the ritual of the church, in which he aßeged that the vicar was not acting in accordance with the law in regard to his conduct of the services, sad said that he was not carrying out fully the four points upon which the bishop had directed him. The Vicar said that was an accusation and the Bishop of Liverpool would settle it. Mr. Robinson said he should he sorry to make an ac- cusation. He Believed he had a conscientious duty a perform in preventing the church being carried over o the Church of Rome, and be did not think the Vic ar ought to be unkind to them for trying. He could not see his way to accept the position of warden for the I next 12 months. He proposed Mr. Hilton, and 61r. W. I Marsh seconded.

The Vicar said that was a new parish and the church- warden must be a communicant of the parish. That was the law of the land, and that was his objection to the nomination.

Mr. Robinson : If you make that objection seriously, I shall move the adjournment of the meetinz until the opinion of the Bishop's Secretary can be obtained. The Vicar : That is my ruling. Mr. Robinson. Then I move that this meeting be adjournod to a future date, until decision is received upon the objection raised. Mr. W. Marsh seconded and the nation was unani- mously carried.

RENEWED BOMBARDMENT OF SANTIAGO. . Tuesday's attack on Santi igo does not appear to have done much damage, and was probably nothing more than a reconnaisance of the apnroaehee to Santiago. . NEW YORE, THURSDAY. TWorld "publishes a despatch from ita eerrhese "Eveningoon dentat Port-au-Prince staling that according to advices received there from Santiago the American squadron made a renewed attack upon the forts at tbe entrance to the bay this morning. The town was In a state of panic' and it was feared that at any moment alh assault would be made by 3,000 insurgents on land aS well as by the Americans frornthe sea. More mines have been laid down its the channel. A later telegram says it is reported from Santiago that the rebels from Siguano camp attacked the city in three columns from Caopo Real. San Antonio, and Cani,and that a bloody and deterneirsed fight was proceeding when the despatch was sent off. RAILWAY DISASTER AT LEYLAND. THREE PERSONS KILLED AND TWENTY INJURED. A disastrous collision to place at Leyland on the joint Lancashire mail Yorkshire and London and North-Western Railway at nine o'clock last night.' As far as can be ascertained, two excursion trains were proceeding from Blackpool to Manchester. The first was blocked at Leyland. The second train crashed into the tail end with terrible effect. As quickly as possible effective aid was rendered. It was found that three persons had been killed outright, and some twenty in- , jured. Fifteen of the injured were conveyed to the Railway Hotel, and afterwards sent forward te Preston. One of the passengers, Mrs. Renshaw, the wife of William Henry Renshaw, of 52, Piiilios Park-road, Manchester, told a pitiable story. She said she belor,ged, like trio other travellers by the ill-fated train, to the. Canning-etreet Temperance Society. After a pro 'sant day at Blackpool they left on the return j am-net, at four minutes past eight. On nearing Leyland station the train drew up, and her hue- band and one or two pessengere looked out of the win- dew to See if they ceull ascertain the reason. One of them had hardly resumed his seat when there was a tre- mendous crash, and thee were dashed villently about. We were in the last carriage but one," continued Mrs. Renshaw (whose bead was all bandaged, her cheek be- ing badly bruised, and the right earring having been literally cut trorn her ear), "and the sheuk threw us all in a heap. ' Oas of my little girls I nicked up and threw out through the window as well as I could. She was all covered with blood. Another of the children was flung out of the carriage in some way, and we found her in a ditch by the aisle of the line. My husband is very badly hurt, I am afraid, and he has been kept back and sent to Preston Infirmary. I shall never forget it. When the collision occurred our carriage seemed to he all broken Into smithereens." Mrs. Renshaw and the children Svhona she bad brought with her were sent home in a cab. _

Mr. Ste ptoe, traveller, 29, Plymmath-street, Lower Broughton,whose bead was bound up in a blond-stained hankerchief, said there was a terrific crash, and we were all flung from our seats. In some way or other we got out of the carriage, and then we found that we had run into a Blackpool excursion. I don't kaow exactly how many were ki:led, but I was told that at least three passengers had been fatally injured. One poor young • fellow who is among the killed had only been married a few weeks. One woman I aw appoared to have both legs broken and her back injured. GREAT FIRE A e PESgAWUR. 8011B9Y, THURSDAY.
